\begin{abstract}
Sparse high-dimensional signal recovery is only possible under certain conditions on the number of parameters, sample size, signal strength and underlying sparsity. We show that leveraging external information, as possible with data integration or transfer learning, allows to push these mathematical limits. Specifically, we consider external information that allows splitting parameters into blocks, first in a simplified case, the Gaussian sequence model, and then in the general linear regression setting. We show how external information dependent, block-based, $\ell_0$ penalties attain model selection consistency under milder conditions than standard $\ell_0$ penalties, and they also attain faster model recovery rates. We first provide results for oracle-based $\ell_0$ penalties that have access to perfect sparsity and signal strength information. Subsequently, we propose an empirical Bayes data analysis method that does not require oracle information and for which efficient computation is possible via standard MCMC techniques.

Our results provide a mathematical basis to justify the use of data integration methods in high-dimensional structural learning.

% \david{Below a possible abstract update}
% \color{blue}
% Sparse high-dimensional signal recovery is only possible under certain conditions on the number of parameters, sample size, signal strength and underlying sparsity (number of truly non-zero parameters). We show that these mathematical limits can be pushed when one has external information that allow splitting parameters into blocks. This occurs in many applications, including data integration and transfer learning tasks. We consider the Gaussian sequence model and linear regression, and show how block-based criteria attain model selection consistency under milder conditions than standard criteria, and they also attain faster model recovery rates. We first provide results for oracle-based criteria that have access to perfect sparsity and signal strength information, and subsequently an empirical Bayes-motivated block L0 penalty that does not require oracle information.

\section{Introduction} 

High-dimensional inference theory relies on assumptions regarding sparsity and signal strength which,
although mathematically necessary, can be too strong in practice (e.g., see \cite{giannone2021economic}).
Our motivation is that in many applications one has external information regarding each parameter, e.g. its magnitude or its likelihood of being non-zero, that can be leveraged to relax said assumptions and enhance inference.
%In many modern applications, the sample size is insufficient to reliably do inference. This limitation has motivated the development of high-dimensional techniques that frequently rely on strict assumptions on sparsity and signal strength. Yet, often, external information is available and can be leveraged to enhance inference.
%Our motivation stems from the observation that, often, 
In particular, external information can guide our decisions regarding which parameters to include in a regression model. 
In a data integration setting, this information originates from previous datasets or similar selection problems (e.g., studying related cancer types). More generally, the information may also originate from each variable's inherent nature (e.g., clinical history vs. genomic markers, sociodemographics versus job history), or meta-covariates (e.g., functional annotations on genes), etc.
%This information may originate from each variable's inherent nature (e.g., clinical history versus genomic markers, sociodemographics versus job history),
%information from previous datasets or similar selection problems (e.g., studying related cancer types), or meta-covariates
%or external data that distinguishes relevant variables from irrelevant ones 
%(e.g., functional annotations on genes), for example. %, \paul{or from similar selection problems (e.g. transfer learning)}. 
We investigate this concept in the Gaussian sequence model and in linear regression, where external information partitions variables into blocks with potentially distinct characteristics. We show that said information allows pushing the mathematical conditions under which consistent model recovery is possible, and improving the associated rates.

Using external information is often advocated within the data integration and transfer learning literature, as joint learning can lead to more accurate inference than analyzing datasets separately. Indeed, numerous applied works employed external information to guide inference. For instance, \cite{stingo:2011, cassese:2014} proposed Bayesian variable selection methods for gene expression, where prior probabilities for non-zero coefficients depend on biological knowledge and meta-covariates. Additionally, \cite{chen_tinghuei:2021} predicted disease outcomes by allowing LASSO penalties to depend on functional annotation categories. Beyond regression, node and edge covariates have been incorporated in \cite{peterson} and \cite{jewson2023graphical} to drive edge inclusion in Gaussian graphical models, while \cite{schiavon2022generalized} used meta-covariates to determine non-zero loadings in factor models. In causal analysis, the inclusion of control covariates may be driven by their degree of association with the covariates of interest (referred to as treatments) 
%guide the inclusion of control covariates based on their degree of association with the treatments 
\citep{belloni2014inference,antonelli2021bayesian}. Collectively, empirical evidence consistently demonstrates improved structural learning when suitable external data is integrated. However, despite this empirical success, a theoretical framework explaining precisely why and how this occurs is not currently available.

%We show that incorporating external information allows pushing the theoretical limits under which consistent variable selection is possible, understood as recovering the true model with probability going to 1 as the sample size $n$ and the number of variables $p$ grow. 
Previous literature extensively explored how high-dimensional variable selection is constrained by inherent characteristics of the data, such as the number of samples $n$ and parameters $p$, the signal strength, the correlation between variables and the number of variables truly associated to the outcome \citep{buhlmannvandegeerbook,handbookbayesvarsel,wainwrighthighdimbook}. Here we analyse how conditions for consistency can be relaxed in the presence of external information. To make our ideas concrete, consider variable selection in the Gaussian linear regression
\begin{equation}\label{eq:linearmodel}
	\bs y \;=\; \bs X \bs \beta^* + \bs\epsilon,\qquad \bs \epsilon \sim N(0,\sigma^2 I_n),
\end{equation}
where $\bs X\in \R^{n\times p}$, $\bs y\in \R^n$, $\sigma >0$, and $\bs \beta \in \mathbb{R}^p$ are the data-generating parameters. A large class of methods operate by penalizing the size of an estimated $\hat{\bs  \beta}$. For instance, penalized likelihood methods optimize the log-likelihood plus a penalty term driven by the $\ell_q$ "norm" of $\hat{\bs  \beta}$ for $q\in [0,1]$  or folded concave penalties \citep{lasso,bertsimas}. 
In Bayesian settings, variable selection is often based on posterior model probabilities that are directly connected to $\ell_0$ penalties \citep{BIC,EBIC,rossell2022concentration}. 

Our interest is in the setting where the external information partitions the $p$ variables into $b$ blocks denoted $B_j \subset \{1,\ldots,p\}$, $j=1,\ldots,b$ such that key characteristics, like the level of sparsity or signal strength, is thought to potentially vary across the $B_j$'s. Assume that for each variable $i=1,\ldots,n$ we have external information $z_i$ that partitions variables into $b$ blocks,
%we have access to a vector of external information $z_i$ that partitions variables $i=1,\ldots,p$ into $b$ blocks, 
i.e. a partition function 
$$\Pi: z_i \to B_j \in \{1,...b\}.$$ 
We consider $\ell_0$ penalties that depend on the external information $\bs z=(z_1,\ldots,z_p)$ through the induced partition. More precisely, we introduce block $\ell_0$ penalties that allow modulating the strength of the penalty in each block. Unlike standard $\ell_0$ penalties, such as BIC \citep{BIC} or EBIC \citep{EBIC}, block penalties are non-exchangeable in the sense that the penalty for adding a variable $i$ may depend on its block $B_j=B_j(z_i)$. 

Our focus on $\ell_0$ penalties is motivated by their superior variable selection properties (e.g. see \cite{infotheowainwright}) %XXXX \paul{Do you have good references that would discuss that David? I am not finding them} 
which makes them particularly suitable to investigate the benefits of incorporating external information. 
Although our goal is to study fundamental properties of structural learning,
we remark that advances in optimization and MCMC methods made $\ell_0$ penalization more computationally tractable: it can be solved exactly for $p$ in the hundreds \citep{bertsimas} and with probability going to 1 with linear cost in $p$ using MCMC \citep{Yang2016,zhou2022} (under mild conditions). Moreover, our results in the Gaussian sequence model apply to essentially any penalized likelihood or Bayesian method, including $\ell_1$ penalties, because in that setting selection operates by thresholding \citep{davidomiros}. 

We mainly discuss external informed penalties that grow linearly in the model size. The linearity assumption allows useful connections with $\ell_1$ penalties and Bayesian methods. We show that our results are tight with respect to known limits on exact support recovery \citep{butucea2018,infotheowainwright}. Nonlinear $\ell_0$ penalties have however been shown to be optimal for estimation and prediction in the Gaussian sequence model \citep{Wu2013} %\paul{That's Harrisson Zhou...} 
and linear regression \citep{Bunea2007}. In the Supplement, we give support recovery guarantees and rates of convergence for nonlinear, externally informed, block penalties.  
\smallskip

\noindent \textbf{Our contributions:} 
%Our main contributions are as follows. 
We show that external information-dependent, block-based, $\ell_0$ penalties soften the theoretical conditions for consistent model recovery understood as recovering the support of $\bs \beta^*$ %exactly the true model 
with probability going to 1 as $n$ and $p$ grow. We consider first the sequence model, a simplified setting where we obtain very tight results. %which simplicity allows to capture the essence of the benefits of external information-dependent penalties. 
These relate to existing literature, our goal is to characterize precisely the benefits of external information.
Second, we study 
%We then extend our results to 
linear regression under arbitrary design, which is our main contribution. In both settings, we
show that an oracle may take advantage of the external information %to set block-based penalties 
so that variable selection consistency is either attained where otherwise it would not be possible, or is attained at a faster rate. Our analysis highlights in particular how leveraging external information weakens (potentially overly stringent) conditions on signal strength for support recovery.
Finally, we propose empirical Bayes data-analysis  %variable selection 
procedures that realize the theoretical benefits without requiring an oracle
(see \cite{petrone:2014,castillo2020spike} for background on empirical Bayes in Bayesian model selection). 
In our examples, these methods run in seconds using MCMC. Another contribution of independent interest are new tight necessary and sufficient conditions for variable selection consistency in linear regression under arbitrary design and fixed support.
\smallskip

\noindent \textbf{Related work:} Our work has connections with multiple hypothesis testing ideas. In this line of research, \cite{genovese2006} proposed a false discovery rate (FDR) procedure in which p-values are weighted based on prior information. They show that if the weights are positively associated to the null hypotheses being false, their procedure improves power. Subsequent works discussed oracle choices of weights either based on external information or derived from the data \citep{roederwasserman,basu}. Recently, \cite{ramdas2019prior} included prior information in a group-based FDR control. 
Relative to this work, we study variable selection consistency in high-dimensional regression as well as the associated conditions on sparsity and signal strength.

Also related to our work, \cite{scarlett2012compressed} studied compressed sensing when prior information allows splitting parameters into blocks, where one knows the true proportion of non-zero parameters in each block. They show that sparse signal recovery with block-based penalties requires smaller $n$ than with exchangeable penalties. A key difference with our work is their assuming independence across covariates, which renders the results inapplicable to regression. Also, they do not consider the sequence model, nor that the proportions of non-zero parameters are unknown in practice.
\smallskip

\noindent \textbf{Organization:} %The paper is organized as follows. 
Section~\ref{sec:intro_blockpen} introduces block $\ell_0$ penalization. Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth} presents its
model selection properties and benefits in the Gaussian sequence model, in an oracle setting where the true sparsity and betamin conditions are known for all blocks. Section~\ref{sec:properties_legression} studies block $\ell_0$ penalties in linear regression, and shows analogous benefits to those in Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth}. These results can be extended to a wide class of Bayesian variable selection methods. Section~\ref{sec:informed_l0} presents data-based 
procedures, motivated by empirical Bayes, that achieve the improved model selection consistency and rates without requiring an oracle. Section~\ref{sec:simulation} shows empirical examples and Section~\ref{sec:discussion} concludes. Proofs are gathered in the Supplement.
\smallskip

\noindent \textbf{Notation:}  
We denote by $\bs \beta\in \R^p$ the parameters of interest 
and by $\bs \beta^*$ their true values. Let  $V=\{1,\ldots,p\}$. For any $A\subseteq V$ and any vector $\bs x\in \R^p$, $\bs x_A$ denotes the subvector of $\bs x$ with entries corresponding to indices in $A$. For any matrix $\bs X \in \R^{n\times p}$, $\bs X_A$ denotes the submatrix of $\bs X$ obtained by selecting the \emph{columns} with indices in $A$. We denote by
$S=\left\{i\in V:\,\,  \beta^*_i \neq 0 \right\}$ the true support of $\bs \beta^*$, its size is $s=|S|$, and hence $p-s$ is the number of truly inactive parameters.
Denote $\beta_{\min}^*=\min_{i \in S}|\beta^*_i|$ the smallest true signal.
We assume that $V$ is partitioned into a fixed number $b$ of disjoint blocks $B_j \subseteq V$ for $j=1,\ldots,b$.
We denote by $S_j=\left\{i\in B_j:\,\,  \beta^*_i \neq 0\right\}$ the set of active parameters in block $B_j$,  $s_j=|S_j|$ its size, $p_j-s_j$ the number of inactive parameters in the block,
and $\beta_{\min,j}^*=\min_{i \in S_j}|\beta^*_i|$.
Given sequences $f(n) > 0$ and $g(n) >0$, $f(n)=O(g(n))$ means that there exists a constant $c<\infty$ such that $f(n) \leq c g(n)$ for all $n \geq n_0$ and some fixed $n_0$, $f(n)=o(g(n))$ means that $\lim_{n\to\infty}f(n)/g(n)=0$, and $f(n)=\Theta(g(n))$ means that $f(n) = O(g(n))$ and $g(n) = O(f(n))$. For any set $A$, $A^C$ denotes the complement of $A$. \smallskip

\noindent\textbf{Our asymptotic regime:} Although not explicitly denoted, $s_j$, $p_j$ and $\beta_{\min,j}^*$ are functions of $n$, and so are $s$, $p$ and $\beta_{\min}^*$. We study asymptotic regimes where \\[5pt]
     \hypertarget{lab:A1}{(A1)}\quad $n \,\to \, \infty$, and the number of blocks $b$ is constant. \\[5pt]
     \hypertarget{lab:A2}{(A2)}\quad For all $j$, $p_j-s_j \,\to \, \infty$.\\[5pt]
Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1} can be slightly relaxed to allow $b$ to grow slowly with $p$, but we assume a constant $b$ for simplicity.
Similarly, Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2} is a mild assumption that can be relaxed but simplifies the exposition. We provide results for both the case where the $s_j\geq1$ are fixed and $s_j \rightarrow \infty$. We do not make any assumption on the asymptotic regime linking $n$ and $p$, but our main interest is in $n= o(p)$ settings. Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}--\hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2} describe the general framework of our results, in each of our results below we specify precisely what %we precise for each result which 
assumptions are needed.
%Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2} implies that $p-s$ goes to infinity but we do not make any assumption on the asymptotic regime linking $n$ and $p$. We provide results for both cases $s_j\geq1$ fixed or diverging.

\section{Variable selection via informed block penalization}\label{sec:intro_blockpen}

Consider a set of candidate models $\M$ given by subsets $M\subseteq V$ and their corresponding coordinate subspaces 
%\david{I'm confused, we just said $\M$ but below we have $\mathcal{L}_M$? Should it be $\M$ everywhere?} \paul{$\M$ is the set of models, $\mathcal{L}_M$ is the parameter space for any $\hat{\beta}_M$: $\hat{\beta}_i$ is 0 if $i\not \in M$, it is any real number if $i\not \in M$}
$$
\mathcal{L}_M\;:=\;\{\beta\in \R^p: \beta_i=0\mbox{ if }i\notin M\}.
$$
Consider a standard $\ell_0$ selection procedure with penalty $\eta(M)$ that depends on $M$ linearly through its cardinality, $\eta(M)=\kappa |M|$ for some $\kappa>0$. The selected model is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stdl0}
\hat{S}= \arg\max_{M\in \M} \left\{\max_{\beta\in \mathcal{L}_M}\ell(\bs y; \bs \beta)\;-\;\kappa |M|\right\},
\end{equation}
where $\ell(\bs y; \bs \beta)$ is the log-likelihood function. 
We study a externally-informed block procedure that penalizes differently the blocks $B_1,\ldots,B_b$ induced by the external information,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Mhat}
  \hat S^b\;\in\;\arg\max_{M\in \M} \left\{\max_{\beta\in \mathcal{L}_M}\ell(\bs y; \bs \beta)-\sum_{j=1}^b\kappa_j |M_j| \right\},
\end{equation}
where $\kappa_1>0,\ldots,\kappa_b>0$. We denote a model by $M=M_1\cup\cdots\cup M_b$,
where $M_j \subseteq B_j$ are %and with $M_j:=M\cap B_j$ represents 
the selected parameters in block $j$.
Note that $\hat{S}^1$ is the standard $\ell_0$ selector in \eqref{eq:stdl0}.

\section{Gaussian sequence model}\label{sec:properties_orth}

In this section we discuss the properties of the externally-informed $\hat S^b$ in the sequence model. It is a popular simplified model for high-dimensional inference, and it is also central to non-parametric statistics. See \cite{johnstone}, Chapter 3 for a complete introduction. In our case, the study of the sequence model allows to capture the essence of the benefits of $\hat S^b$ over standard selectors before moving to the setting of interest, linear regression.

We start by introducing the sequence model, its connection to orthogonal linear regression and discuss that $\hat S^b$ simplifies to performing block-based thresholding. %performs block-wise thresholding in that setting. 
We then provide the variable selection properties of $\hat S^b$, discuss its benefits in lessening the conditions for consistent variable selection and in its convergence rates, and illustrate said advantages under different asymptotic regimes.

In this section, we focus on regimes where, in addition to Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}--\hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2}, we have\\[5pt]
%we study the selection properties of $\hat{S}^b$ under assumption \\[5pt]
\hypertarget{lab:A3}{(A3)}\quad $\text{For all } j, \;\; s_j \,\to \, \infty$.\\[5pt]
That is, we assume a diverging number of active signals $s_j$ in every block. %Alternatively, the $s_j$'s can be assumed to be finite. 
We present here results for $\hat S^b$ under Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A3}{A3} to simplify the exposition, and because we derive a novel necessary condition on signal strength under that assumption. We obtained analogous results when the $s_j$'s are finite (section S5 of the Supplement). Note some results in the current section do not require Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A3}{A3}, we specify in each result which assumptions are needed. 

\subsection{Sequence model and thresholding}\label{sec:GSMgen}

The Gaussian sequence model assumes:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GSM}
	\bs y \;=\; \sqrt{n} \bs \beta^* + \bs\epsilon,\qquad \bs \epsilon \sim N(0,\sigma^2 I_p),
\end{equation}
where $\bs y \in \mathbb{R}^p$ and without loss of generality we set $\sigma^2=1$ to streamline notation. 

Let $\tilde{\bs \beta}=\bs y/\sqrt{n} \sim N(\bs \beta^*,\tfrac{1}{n}I_p)$ be the MLE under \eqref{eq:GSM}. The next basic result states that %, by orthogonality, 
the block-wise $\ell_0$ penalty reduces to thresholding $\tilde{\bs \beta}$, %$\tilde{\bs \beta}=(\tilde \beta_i)$, 
with block-dependent thresholds.
\begin{prop}\label{prop:isthresh}
	In the sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, let $\hat{S}^b$ and $\kappa_1,\ldots,\kappa_b$ defined in \eqref{eq:Mhat}. Then $\hat S^b=\hat S^b_1\cup \cdots \cup \hat S^b_b$, where, for each $j=1,\ldots,b$,
	$$
	\hat S_j^b\;:=\;\left\{i\in B_j:\; |\tilde{\beta}_i|> \sqrt{\frac{2\kappa_j}{n}}\right\}.
	$$ 
\end{prop}
Other popular penalties
%Most popular variable selection procedures 
also take this block-based thresholding form. It is the case of the LASSO and adaptive LASSO when letting the penalty vary by block: see Lemma~S1.1 %in Auxiliary results 
in the Supplement. %Auxiliary results in the Supplement~S0. 
It is also the case of Bayesian procedures under most standard priors \citep{davidomiros} where one sets a different prior inclusion probability in each block. Thus, equivalently, to study the block-informed penalization for the Gaussian sequence model, we study generic thresholding model selectors of the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thresholdS}
\hat S_j^{b}\;:=\;\{i\in B_j: |\tilde{\beta}_i|>\tau_j\}, \qquad\bs \tau=(\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_b)\in \R^b_{>0}.	
\end{equation}

Results in this section also generalize to orthogonal linear regression with normalized columns, i.e. when $\bs X$ in \eqref{eq:linearmodel} satisfies $\bs X^\top \bs X=n I_p$. In that setting the MLE is also distributed $N(\bs \beta^*,\tfrac{1}{n}I_p)$ and %by orthogonality, 
variable selection with $\hat{S}^b$ also amounts to block thresholding.

\subsection{Selection based on block thresholds}
\label{ssec:block_thresholds}


We study here the statistical performance of the block thresholding operator in \eqref{eq:thresholdS}. Block thresholding was previously studied in the context of parameter estimation for wavelet-based models with equally sized blocks; see \cite{johnstone}, Chapters 7 to 9.
We focus here on its variable selection properties, for arbitrarily-sized blocks. We successively analyze properties relative to conditions for recovery and rates of convergence. 

Consider the following assumptions,\\
\hypertarget{lab:A4}{(A4)}\quad for all $j=1,\ldots,b$ and every sufficiently large $n$,\;\; $\sqrt{n}\tau_j \;\geq\;  \sqrt{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}$,\\[5pt]
\hypertarget{lab:A5}{(A5)}\quad for all $j=1,\ldots,b$ and 
every sufficiently large $n$, \;
	 $\sqrt{n}(\beta_{\min,j}^* - \tau_j) \;\geq\; \sqrt{2\ln(s_j)}$.

\begin{prop}\label{prop:suffnecblockthresh} In the
sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, assume \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}, \hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2} and \hyperlink{lab:A3}{A3}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item If Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A4}{A4} holds, then 
$\lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} P(\hat{S}^{b}\subseteq S) = 1$.
\item If for some $j\in\{1,\ldots,b\}$\; $
\lim_{n\to \infty} \sqrt{n}\tau_j/\sqrt{2\ln(p_j-s_j)} \;<\;1$,
then $\lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} P(\hat{S}^{b}\subseteq S ) <1$.
\item If Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} holds, then
$\lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} P(\hat{S}^{b}\supseteq  S)= 1$.
\item Suppose there exists $j\in \{1,\ldots,b\}$ such that  $\beta^*_i=\beta_{\min,j}^*$ for all $i \in S_j$, $s_j/p_j<1$, and $\lim_{n\to \infty} \sqrt{n}\tau_j/\sqrt{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}\geq1$. If, in addition, $\lim_{n\to \infty} \sqrt{n}(\beta_{\min,j}^*-\tau_j)/\sqrt{(\pi/2)\ln(s_j)}\leq 1$
then $\lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} P(\hat S^{b}\supseteq S ) < 1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}

Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecblockthresh} (i) to (iii) extend to $\hat{S}^{b}$ results known for the standard thresholding $\hat{S}$ ($\hat{S}^b$ with $b=1$) in orthogonal linear regression \citep{ slope, buhlmannvandegeerbook, wainwrighthighdimbook} and in the sequence model \citep{johnstone}, Chapter 3. Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecblockthresh} (iv) gives a new necessary condition on signal strength for support recovery in probability in a worst case in which all signals are equal. It shows that in that case sufficient Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} is essentially necessary up to a constant factor (replacing 2 by $\pi/2$).

% By Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecIblockthresh},
% a necessary assumption to have no false positives asymptotically
% is
% \begin{equation}\label{cond:necIallblockthresh}
% \lim_{n\to \infty} \frac{\tau_j}{\sqrt{\tfrac{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}{n}}}\;\geq\;1\qquad\mbox{for all }j=1,\ldots,b.	
% \end{equation}

% \bigskip

% \noindent\textbf{False negatives.} 
% False negatives occur when $\hat S^{b}\not\supseteq S$, i.e. for some block $j \in \{1,\ldots,b\}$ and parameter $i\in B_j$,  we have that $\beta^*_i\neq 0$ and $|\tilde\beta_i|\leq \tau_j$.
% Control of false negatives in a block $B_j$ requires bounding the minimum of $s_j=|S_j|$ independent Gaussians with non-zero means $\beta^*_i$, $i\in S_j$. This can be achieved by setting a betamin condition
% on $\beta_{\min,j}^*\;:=\;\min\{|\beta^*_i|: i\in S_j\}$.  
% Intuitively, to have no false negatives (also known as screening property), each $\tau_j$ needs to be smaller than $\beta^*_{\min,j}$.
% More precisely, a sufficient assumption is: \\[5pt]


% \begin{prop}\label{prop:suffnecIIblockthresh}
%  In the sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, assume \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}--\hyperlink{lab:A3}{A3} and \hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5}. Then
% $\lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} P(\hat{S}^{b}\supseteq  S)= 1$.
% \end{prop}
% Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} was proved to be sufficient for selection consistency in orthogonal linear regression in \cite{wainwrighthighdimbook} for standard thresholding. Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecIIblockthresh} generalizes the result to block-thresholding. The study of {\it necessary} assumptions
% is more complicated. Proposition~\ref{prop:necIIblockthresh} below provides first a necessary condition valid for any vector $\bs \beta^*$, and second, a tight condition for a worst-case scenario where $\beta_i^*=\beta_{\min}^*$ for all $i\in S$.


% \bigskip

% \noindent\textbf{Exact model recovery guarantees.} 
Combining Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A4}{A4} (Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecblockthresh} (i)) and Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} (Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecblockthresh} (iii)), we asymptotically recover the correct support if for all $j=1,\ldots,b$,
\begin{equation*}
  \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}{n}}\;\;\leq\;\;\tau_j\;\;\leq\;\;\beta_{\min,j}^*-\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_j)}{n}}
\end{equation*}
for every sufficiently large $n$. In particular, this requires that for all $j=1,\ldots,b$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betamin}
    \beta_{\min,j}^* \geq \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}{n}} + \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_j)}{n}}.
\end{equation}
% By Propositions~\ref{prop:suffnecIblockthresh} and~\ref{prop:suffnecIIblockthresh} signals as small as the right-hand side in \eqref{eq:betamin} can be recovered in block $B_j$. 
Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecblockthresh} (ii) and (iv) show that Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A4}{A4}--\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} are essentially necessary. It follows that betamin condition \eqref{eq:betamin} is near-necessary for selection consistency, in the following sense.
\begin{lem}\label{lem:necbetaminblockthresh}
    If there exists a block $j\in\{1,\ldots,b\}$ with equal non-zero parameter values $\beta^*_i=\beta_{\min,j}^*$ for all $i \in S_j$, where $0<s_j/p_j<1$ and 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:necbetaminblockthresh}
        \lim_{n\to \infty}\frac{\sqrt{n}\beta_{\min,j}^*}{\sqrt{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}+\sqrt{(\pi/2)\ln(s_j)}}\;<\; 1,
    \end{equation}
    then under Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}--\hyperlink{lab:A3}{A3},\;  $\lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} P(\hat{S}^{b} = S) < 1$.
\end{lem}
For standard thresholding $\hat{S}$, \cite{butucea2018} showed that the complementary of \eqref{eq:necbetaminblockthresh} where in the denominator $\pi/2$ is replaced by $2$ is strictly necessary for a stricter definition of selection consistency, the vanishing in expectation of the Hamming loss (the number of false negatives and positives). 

\smallskip

\noindent\textbf{Rate of convergence.} Building upon the
preceding results, we bound the rate of convergence of $P(\hat S^{b}\neq S)$.
\begin{thm}\label{theo:fullrecovGS}
Assume \hyperlink{lab:A4}{A4} and \hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} and that for all $j=1,\ldots,b$, $p_j-s_j>1$ and $s_j>1$. %\david{Clarify: also \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}-\hyperlink{lab:A3}{A3}?} \paul{No, \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}-\hyperlink{lab:A3}{A3} are not required for the bound to hold} 
Then, for every sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{equation} \label{eq:rateconvgsm}
P(\hat{S}^{b} \neq S)\;\leq\;\sum_{j=1}^b\,\,\frac{e^{-\tfrac{n}{2}\big[\tau_j^2-\tfrac{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}{n}\big]}}{\sqrt{\pi\ln(p_j-s_j)}}+\frac{e^{-\tfrac{n}{2}\big[(\beta^*_{\min,j}-\tau_j)^2-\tfrac{2\ln(s_j)}{n}\big]}}{\sqrt{\pi\ln(s_j)}}.
\end{equation}
Further,
if $\beta^*_{\min,j}$ satisfy \eqref{eq:betamin} for every block $j$, and the thresholds $\tau_j$ take oracle values
	\begin{equation} \label{eq:optimtauj}
\tau^*_j \;=\; \frac{\beta^*_{\min,j}}{2}+\frac{\ln({p_j/s_j}-1)}{n\beta^*_{\min,j}},
\end{equation}
then $\tau^*_j$ satisfy Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A4}{A4} and \hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} and, for every sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:optimrateconvgsm}
    P(\hat{S}^{b} \neq S) \; \leq \; 2 \sum_{j=1}^be^{-[\frac{n}{8}{\beta^*_{\min,j}}^2-\ln \max\{p_j-s_j,s_j\}]}.
\end{equation}
\end{thm}
In \eqref{eq:rateconvgsm} the first term of each summand decreases exponentially in $n\tau_j^2/2$, while the second term decreases exponentially in $n(\beta_{\min,j}^*-\tau_j)^2/2$. The choice $\tau_j=\tau^*_j$ ensures that both terms are equal and hence approximates the values minimizing \eqref{eq:rateconvgsm}. We refer to these ideal $\tau^*_j$ as {\it oracle thresholds} because they depend on quantities $s_j$ and $\beta_{\min,j}$ that are unknown in practice. 
The bound in \eqref{eq:optimrateconvgsm} closely approximates \eqref{eq:rateconvgsm} for $\tau_j=\tau^*_j$ and is tightest in the worst-case scenario where $\beta_i^*=\beta_{\min,j}^*$ for all $i \in S_j$ and all $j$, in that it approximates the fastest rate achievable in that worst case. In fact, for standard thresholding $\hat{S}$ ($\hat{S}^b$ with $b=1$), Corollary 2.1 of \cite{butucea2018} shows that the choice of threshold $\tau^*=\beta^*_{\min}/2+\ln({p/s-1})/(n\beta^*_{\min})$ is minimax for the Hamming loss up to a constant factor smaller than 2. By independence, it is straightforward that $\tau_j^*$ is minimax, up to a factor 2, for the Hamming loss \textit{in block $B_j$}.

\subsection{Benefits of block thresholds}\label{sec:prosconsblock}

We now examine the benefits of block thresholds. We discuss
two types of benefits: softening the 
conditions for model selection consistency and improving the associated convergence rates. \smallskip

% Intuitively, block penalties allow adapting 
% to varying sparsity $s_j$  or signal strengths $\beta^*_{\min,j}$ across blocks. 
% When the number of inactive parameters $p_j-s_j$ is small one may use smaller thresholds $\tau_j$ and still control false positives, and when $\beta^*_{\min,j}$ is large one may use larger $\tau_j$ and still control false negatives.
% As the blocks become more discriminative, that is, more distinct $p_j-s_j$ and $\beta^*_{\min,j}$ across blocks, the larger the potential benefits.
% Consider a somewhat extreme example with $b=2$ blocks, a large sparse block with $s_1=0$, $p_1-s_1=80$ and a smaller non-sparse block with $s_2=10$, $p_2-s_2=10$, with $n=1$ and $\beta_i^*=4$ for all $i\in S$. 
% In the left panel of Figure~\ref{fig:starter}, a standard threshold selector is used with $\tau=\sqrt{2 \ln(p-s)/ n}$, the minimal threshold such that the family-wise error rate vanishes (dashed line). The large number of truly zero parameters $p-s=90$ forces a high threshold, which leads to a false negative. In the right panel, block thresholds are set to $\tau_j=\sqrt{2 \ln(p_j-s_j)/ n}$.
% Adjusting $\tau_2$ to the lower number of truly zero means in block 2 ($p_2-s_2$) allows recovering the true model. 
% \begin{figure}[ht]
% \begin{minipage}{1\columnwidth}
% \begin{tabular}{M{62mm}M{62mm}}
%         Standard thresholding & Block thresholding \\
%          \includegraphics[scale=1]{images/p.intro.std.pdf} & \includegraphics[scale=1]{images/p.intro.block.pdf} \\
% \end{tabular}
% \end{minipage}

Left panel: the dashed line is $\sqrt{2 \ln (p-s)/n}$, the minimal threshold such that the FWER vanishes. Right panel: observations are grouped in two blocks of sizes $p_1=80$ and $p_2=20$. The dashed lines are the minimal block thresholds such that the FWER vanishes}
% \label{fig:starter}
% \end{figure}
% \bigskip

\noindent\textbf{Conditions for variable selection consistency.} 
% In the example, the possibility to lower thresholds in the less sparse blocks %with $\hat S^2$ 
% allowed recovering the smallest active signal. 
% More precisely, 
Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A4}{A4}--\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} give ranges of threshold values that are sufficient and essentially necessary for asymptotic support recovery. For the standard selector $\hat{S}$, the range for the single threshold $\tau$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rangethreshex}
  \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p-s)}{n}}\;\;\leq\;\;\tau\;\;\leq\;\;\beta^*_{\min}-\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s)}{n}}.	
\end{equation}
For the block threshold selector $\hat{S}^{b}$, 
%assuming without loss of generality that the global minimum $\beta_{\min}^*$ is in block $b$, 
the ranges for the $\tau_j$'s are
    \begin{eqnarray}\label{eq:rangeblockthreshex}
  \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}{n}}\;\;&\leq\;\;\tau_j\;\;\leq&\;\;\beta^*_{\min,j}-\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_j)}{n}}   .
%  \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_1-s_1)}{n}}\;\;&\leq\;\;\tau_1\;\;\leq&\;\;\beta^*_{\min,1}-\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_1)}{n}}\nonumber\\
%  &\;\;\vdots \\
%  \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_b-s_b)}{n}}\;\;&\leq\;\;\tau_b\;\;\leq&\;\;\beta^*_{\min}-\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_b)}{n}} \nonumber.       
    \end{eqnarray}

The ranges in \eqref{eq:rangeblockthreshex} imply that $\hat{S}^{b}$ requires milder conditions for selection consistency than $\hat{S}$. Intuitively, if there exist two blocks such that the ranges in \eqref{eq:rangeblockthreshex} do not overlap, 
then a global threshold $\tau$ cannot possibly satisfy \eqref{eq:rangeblockthreshex} for all $j$ and consistent selection is essentially not possible.
For example, this occurs if the global smallest active signal $\beta_{\min}^*$ is in block $b$ and satisfies  $\beta_{\min}^* - \sqrt{(2/n) \ln(s_b)} < \sqrt{(2/n) \ln(p_1-s_1)}$.
More precisely, Corollary~\ref{cor:selconstimpossible} gives
conditions under which consistent selection is possible with $\hat{S}^b$ but not with $\hat{S}$, in a worst-case setting where all non-zero parameters are equal to $\beta_{\min}^*$.


\begin{cor}\label{cor:selconstimpossible}
Assume \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}--\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5}, that $s/p<1$, and $\beta^*_i=\beta_{\min}^*$ for all $i \in S$. If
\begin{eqnarray*}
    &\lim_{n\to \infty}\frac{\beta_{\min}^*}{\sqrt{\tfrac{2\ln(p-s)}{n}}+\sqrt{\tfrac{\pi}{2}\tfrac{\ln(s)}{n}}}\;<\; 1 
\end{eqnarray*}
then 
$\lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} P(\hat{S} = S) < 1$ and $\lim_{n \rightarrow \infty} P(\hat{S}^{b} = S)=1$.
\end{cor}

\noindent\textbf{Rates of convergence.} We just saw that block penalties can attain consistent model selection where standard penalties cannot. We now discuss differences in the probability of correct selection, when consistent selection is possible for both procedures. We assume that block thresholds 
are set to their oracle values $\tau_j^*$ in \eqref{eq:optimtauj} 
and similarly that the single threshold
takes its oracle value $\tau^*$ defined in an analogous way.
Let ${OR}^b_{orth}$ be the oracle convergence rate for $\tau_j^*$ in \eqref{eq:optimrateconvgsm} and ${OR}_{orth}$ that for $\tau^*$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ratioCR}
    \frac{{OR}^b_{orth}}{{OR}_{orth}}\;=\;\sum_{j=1}^be^{-\frac{n}{8}\left({\beta^*_{\min,j}}^2-{\beta_{\min}^*}^2\right)-(\ln \max\{p-s,s\}-\ln \max\{p_j-s_j,s_j\})}
\end{equation}
Since $\beta_{\min,j}^* \geq \beta_{\min}^*$ and $\ln \max\{p-s,s\}\geq\ln \max\{p_j-s_j,s_j\}\big)$, equation \eqref{eq:ratioCR} shows that for every $n$ large enough ${OR}^b_{orth} \leq {OR}_{orth}$ for any partition in blocks, i.e. the oracle convergence rate in \eqref{eq:optimrateconvgsm} for $\tau_j^*$ is never worse than for $\tau^*$. The magnitude of the gain depends on how informative the blocks are. %the discriminative power of the blocks.
For any sparse setting where $s_j<p_j-s_j$ for every $j$, assuming without loss of generality that $\beta_{\min,b}^*=\beta_{\min}^*$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:goodorthratio}
    \frac{{OR}^b_{orth}}{{OR}_{orth}}\;=\; \frac{p_b-s_b}{p-s} \,+\,\sum_{j<b}\frac{p_j-s_j}{p-s}e^{-\frac{n}{8}\left({\beta^*_{\min,j}}^2-{\beta_{\min}^*}^2\right)}
\end{equation}
%\paul{this ratio goes to 1 if the betamins are equal across blocks and for some $j$, $p_j-s_j/p-s\to1$ (happens in our Examples). I guess this is due to simplifications in the bounding.}
There are then two sources of improvement in convergence rates: in any block $j$, a smaller number of truly zero means, that is $p_j-s_j< p-s$, or a larger smallest signal, $\beta^*_{\min,j} > \beta_{\min}^*$. Depending on the sparsity of the block, one of the two effects usually prevails. In blocks where $\beta^*_{\min,j} \approx \beta_{\min}^*$, improvement comes mainly from having a smaller number of truly zero means. In those blocks
one sets $\tau^*_j < \tau^*$, so that one may detect smaller signals. 
If
$\beta^*_{\min,j} \gg \beta_{\min}^*$,
one sets $\tau^*_j > \tau^*$
and the probability of false positives is reduced.

In the particular worst case where all the active signals are equal, $\beta^*_{\min,j}=\beta^*_{\min}$ for all $j$, the ratio 
${OR}^b_{orth}/{OR}_{orth}$ in \eqref{eq:goodorthratio} is exactly one. That is, the oracle rate for the $\tau_j^*$ is not better than for $\tau^*$. This is in line with the minimax analysis of \cite{butucea2018} which shows the near-optimality of $\tau^*$ in the worst case. Under the less rigid assumption of unequal $\beta^*_{\min,j}$'s, ${OR}^b_{orth}/{OR}_{orth}$ is however bounded away from 1 in \eqref{eq:goodorthratio} and the oracle rate for the $\tau_j^*$ is strictly better. This highlights the crucial role that varying signal strength plays in the gains with informed thresholds. The latter cannot be only characterized by the entropy of the distribution of sparsity across blocks as in \cite{scarlett2012compressed}. 
% It is shown that $\tau^*$ is near-minimax over a class of so-called separable selectors that do contain block thresholds. However, the minimized maximum risk is that . We observe indeed that in that case , meaning the $\tau^*_j$ do not outperform $\tau^*$. Our analysis suggests that under the more realistic assumption of unequal active signals, thresholds other than $\tau^*$, like the informed $\tau_j^*$, are preferable. The case where all active signals are equal to $\beta^*_{\min}$ also highlights how a non-uniform distribution of sparsity across blocks alone is insufficient to guarantee improvement in rates of convergence.
% That is, one does not expect benefits when the number of non-zero signals and their magnitudes are similar across blocks. This can be also seen from \eqref{eq:goodorthratio}.

\subsection{Examples with two blocks}\label{sec:examples}

We illustrate our results with four concrete examples contemplating different sparsity regimes and informativeness of the blocks, summarized in Table~\ref{tab:examples}. We focus on a sparse setting where $\ln(s)=o(\ln(p-s))$, with two blocks ($b=2$). We assume that $\beta_{\min}^*$, the smallest non-zero $|\beta_i^*|$ is located in block $2$.

Example 4 is a non-discriminative
