\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Smallest signal recoverable.} 
We compare the \emph{smallest signal recoverable} by $\hat S$ and $\hat S^b$ while still being selection consistent. We denote those by $\underline{\beta}_{\min,orth}^{*}$ and $\underline{\beta}_{\min,b}^{*}$ respectively. In Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5}, we set the threshold(s) to the lowest value such that the family-wise error rate (FWER) vanishes (per Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecblockthresh} (i)). Since we assumed that the global minimum $\beta_{\min}^*$ is in block $2$, we get
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betaminbardef}
    \underline{\beta}_{\min,orth}^{*} := \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p-s)}{n}} + \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s)}{n}},\qquad
    \underline{\beta}^{*,b}_{\min,orth} := \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_2-s_2)}{n}} + \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_2)}{n}}
\end{equation} 
%We discuss the ratio $\underline{\beta}^{*,b}_{\min,orth}/\underline{\beta}_{\min,orth}^{*}$ in our four examples. 

The left panel in Figure~\ref{fig:ratios} plots the ratio %of smallest signal recoverable 
$\underline{\beta}^{*,b}_{\min,orth}/\underline{\beta}^{*}_{\min,orth}$. %in our four examples. 
In Example 1, with discriminative blocks, the
smallest signal recoverable with $\hat{S}^{b}$ is asymptotically about 25\% smaller than with the standard selector $\hat{S}$. 
In Example 2 where blocks are even more discriminative, the ratio converges to 0. %: the smallest signal recoverable with $\hat S^b$ is infinitely smaller.
In Example 4, with non-discriminative blocks, the ratio converges to 1, i.e. the benefits in the recoverable signal fade as $n$ grows. 
Example 3 illustrates how highly discriminative blocks can also bring significant benefits in terms of signal recoverable in a regime that is only somewhat sparse.
\begin{figure}[ht]
\begin{minipage}{1\columnwidth}
\begin{tabular}{M{63mm}M{63mm}}
        $\underline{\beta}^{*,b}_{\min,orth}/\underline{\beta}^{*}_{\min,orth}$ & ${OR}^b_{orth}/{OR}_{orth}$ \\
         \includegraphics[scale=1]{images/p.ratio.smallestbeta.pdf} & \includegraphics[scale=1]{images/p.ratio.orconvrate.pdf} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{minipage}
\caption{Ratio of smallest signals recoverable (left) and oracle convergence rates (right) with $\hat S^b$ and with $\hat S$ in Examples 1--4}
\label{fig:ratios}
\end{figure}

\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Oracle convergence rates.} In the right panel of Figure~\ref{fig:ratios}, we plot the ratio ${OR}^b_{orth}/{OR}_{orth}$ in Examples 1 to 3 where blocks are discriminative. We set $\beta_{\min }^*=\beta_{\min,2}^*=1/5$ and $\beta_{\min,1}^*=1.3\beta_{\min }^*$ to guarantee the recovery is possible with both $\hat S$ and $\hat S^b$. Figure~\ref{fig:ratios} shows that in the three examples the convergence is much faster with $\hat S^b$. %The more discriminative the blocks the faster.


\section{High-dimensional linear regression}
\label{sec:properties_legression}

In Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth} we showed that, in the sequence model, block penalties allow consistent model recovery in settings where it is otherwise not possible (e.g. smaller signals), and improves oracle consistency rates. We now extend the results to linear regression. 
We present sufficient and necessary assumptions, 
betamin conditions and rates for the probability of correct selection for the block informed $\ell_0$-penalized selector $\hat S^b$ in \eqref{eq:Mhat}. We also compare the properties of $\hat S^b$ to those of the standard $\ell_0$ selector $\hat S$. 
Whereas the framework is more involved than in the sequence model and the required proof techniques are different, the results remain conceptually the same. 

%Here $V$ is the set of all variables and $p_j-s_j$ and $s_j$ are respectively the numbers of inactive and active variables in block $B_j$.
%\paul{I removed a sentence that said here "Here $V$ is the set of all variables and $p_j-s_j$ and $s_j$ are respectively the numbers of inactive and active variables in block $B_j$." I don't see why we need to repeat it.} 
We require Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1} across the section, whereas we use \hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2} only in results on necessary conditions for variable selection consistency with $\hat S^b$. Our results on sufficient conditions for selection consistency then hold equally for fixed and diverging $p_j-s_j$ and $s_j$. For simplicity, we assume that $\hat{S}^b$ is unique and that one constrains attention to the set of models $\M\subseteq \mathcal P(V)$ (the power set of $V$),
such that
$\bs X_M$ has full column rank for any $M \in \M$, and that the true support $S$ lies in $\M$. Non-uniqueness of $\hat{S}^b$ and non-full rank models can be accommodated though, at the expense of a slightly more involved treatment.

Our analysis relies on a classical connection
between $\ell_0$ penalties and Bayesian variable selection. 
Section \ref{ssec:regression_proof_strategy} reviews this connection and the proof strategy for our results.
The technical nature of the proof precludes a detailed exposition, we instead only present the most important ideas.
In Section \ref{ssec:regression_mainresult} we state our main theorem
on sufficient conditions for variable selection consistency for $\hat{S}^{b}$ and oracle convergence rates. %as we did for the sequence model. 
To assess the tightness of said sufficient conditions, Section~\ref{ssec:neccond_linreg} gives related {\it necessary} conditions.  %assumptions and betamin conditions for variable selection consistency with $\hat{S}^{b}$. 
Section \ref{ssec:regression_gains} discusses the gains of block penalization in further detail. Section \ref{ssec:nobetaminhyp} gives a general convergence result that, when certain betamin conditions do not hold, one still has guarantees of discarding inactive parameters and detecting sufficiently large active parameters.
%for $\hat{S}^{b}$ under no betamin condition which is used in Section \ref{sec:informed_l0}.
The latter result plays an important role for our data-based procedures in Section \ref{sec:informed_l0}. 


\subsection{Proof strategy}
\label{ssec:regression_proof_strategy}


We state a well-known
reformulation of $\hat S^b$ in linear regression \eqref{eq:linearmodel}. For any model $M\in \M$, let $\tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)}= (\bs X_{\!\!M}^\top \bs X_{\!\!M})^{-1}\bs X_{\!\!M}^\top\bs y \in \R^{|M|}$ be the MLE under model $M$, and denote
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CM}
    {\rm C}(M)\;: =\;\tfrac{1}{2}\|\bs X_M\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(M)}\|^2-\sum_{j=1}^b\kappa_j |M_j|\quad\text{and}\quad NC(M)\;:=\;\frac{e^{{\rm C}(M)}}{\underset{M' \in \M}{\sum} e^{{\rm C}(M')}}.
\end{equation}
\begin{lem}\label{lem:useful-reformulation} $\hat S^b$ satisfies $\hat S^b\;\in\;\arg\max_{M\in \M} NC(M)$
\end{lem}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:useful-reformulation}, $\hat S^b$ selects
$M\in \M$ with the largest normalized score $NC(M)$. This normalized score can be understood as a pseudo-posterior probability for model $M$ in a Bayesian variable selection framework \citep{BIC}.
Proposition 1 in \cite{rossell2022concentration}, reproduced below as Lemma~\ref{lem:L0toL1convergence}, proves that the expectation of such posterior probabilities bounds the probability of an incorrect model selection $(\hat S^b\neq S)$. 

\begin{lem}\label{lem:L0toL1convergence}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item 
$P(\hat{S}^{b} \neq S) \,\,\leq \,\, 2 \sum_{M \in \M\setminus \{S\}} \E\left(NC(M)\right).$
\item  For any $M,M'\in\M$, such that $M\neq M'$, 
$NC(M)\;\leq \;\big(1+e^{C(M')-C(M)}\big)^{-1}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lem}
We use  Lemma~\ref{lem:L0toL1convergence} (i) to show the variable selection consistency of $\hat S^b$ by guaranteeing the vanishing, in expectation, of the sum of the normalized scores. Such vanishing bears resemblance to what is known as \emph{strong selection consistency} property \citep{narisettyhe2014}: the concentration of pseudo-posterior model probabilities on the truth $S$. We prove however $L_1$ convergence instead of convergence in probability.
To bound $\E(NC(M))$ for $M\neq S$, by Lemma~\ref{lem:L0toL1convergence} (ii) with $M'=S$, we may instead bound the expectation of a simple function of $C(S)-C(M)$, the pairwise comparison between each $M$ and the data-generating $S$.
This is achieved by noting that, directly by the definition in \eqref{eq:CM}, $C(S)-C(M)=\tfrac12 L_{SM}+\Delta_{MS}$, where for any two models $M,T\subseteq V$, we denote
\begin{equation}\label{eq:DeltaM}
\Delta_{MT}\;:=\;\sum_{j=1}^b \kappa_j(|M_j|-|T_j|) \quad\text{and} \quad L_{TM}\;:=\;\|\bs X_{T}\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(T)}\|^2-\|\bs X_{M}\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(M)}\|^2.
\end{equation}
%and for any two models $Q,M\subseteq V$, we denote 
%$$L_{QM}\;:=\;\|\bs X_{Q}\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(Q)}\|^2-\|\bs X_{M}\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(M)}\|^2.$$
To lower bound $C(S)-C(M)$ we use that $L_{SM}$ can be expressed in terms of chi-squared variables.
%For any over-fitted model $M \supset S$, we have $\Delta_{MS}>0$ and it is well-known that $-L_{SM} \sim \chi_{|M \setminus S|}^2$. Hence, if one sets large enough $\kappa_j$ (and thus $\Delta_{MS}$), then $NC(M)$ vanishes. For non-overfitted models $M\not\supset S$, 
The idea is to take the union model $Q_S=S\cup M$, and to note that $L_{SM}=L_{Q_SM}-L_{Q_SS}$. We may use the next lemma from \cite{rossell2022concentration} to bound $L_{Q_SM}$, $L_{Q_SS}$, and hence also $L_{SM}$.

\begin{lem}\label{lemma:s7}
    Let $M,Q$ be any two nested models such that $M \subseteq Q$. Then
$$
L_{QM}\;=\;\|\bs X_{Q}\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(Q)}\|^2-\|\bs X_{M}\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(M)}\|^2\;\sim\; \chi_{|Q\setminus M|}^2\left(\mu_{Q M}\right), 
$$
where $\chi_{k}^2(\mu)$ denotes, when $\mu>0$, the noncentral chi-squared distribution with $k$ degrees of freedom and noncentrality parameter $\mu$ and, when $\mu=0$, the chi-squared distribution with $k$ degrees of freedom $\chi_{k}^2$. The parameter $\mu_{QM}$ is given by \begin{equation}\label{eq:muQM}
    \mu_{QM}\;\;:=\;\;\|\big(I_n-P_M\big) \bs X_{Q\setminus M} \bs \beta^*_{Q\setminus M}\|^2
\end{equation}
where $P_M=\bs X_{M}\left(\bs X_{M}^{\top} \bs X_{M}\right)^{-1} \bs X_{M}^{\top}$.
\end{lem}

If $M\supset S$ (over-fitted), then $Q_S=M$ and $\beta^*_{Q_S\setminus S}=\beta^*_{M\setminus S}=0$, because any parameter outside the true support $S$ is by definition $0$. Moreover, $-L_{SM}=L_{Q_SS}$ and by Lemma~\ref{lemma:s7}, $-L_{SM}\sim \chi^2_{|Q_S\setminus S|}$ since $\beta^*_{Q_S\setminus S}=0$. We have $\Delta_{MS}>0$ and if one sets large enough $\kappa_j$ (and thus $\Delta_{MS}$), then $C(S)-C(M)$ is also large and $NC(M)$ vanishes. If $M\subset S$ (under-fitted), then $Q_S=S$, $L_{SM}=L_{Q_SM}$, and by Lemma~\ref{lemma:s7}
$L_{SM}\sim \chi^2_{|Q_S\setminus M|}(\mu_{Q_SM})$, which has expectation $|Q_S\setminus M|+\mu_{Q_SM}$.
If the noncentrality parameter $\mu_{Q_SM}$ is large enough, then $L_{SM}$ and $C(S)-C(M)$ are also large, and $NC(M)$ vanishes. Lemma~\ref{lem:boundrho} below shows that large $\mu_{Q_SM}$ can be achieved by setting a betamin condition and an eigenvalue condition on $\bs X$ involving the following quantity  
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:rhoX}
        \rho(\bs X)\;\;=\;\;\min_{M \in \M:\, M\not \supseteq S} \lambda_{\min}\big(\tfrac{1}{n} \boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}^{\top}\left(I_n- P_M\right) \boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}\big).
    \end{equation}
The quantity $\rho(\bs X)$ is nonnegative and relates to how distinguishable the other models $M\in \M$ are from $S$ \citep{infotheowainwright}.  More specifically, $\tfrac{1}{n} \boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}^{\top}\left(I_n- P_M\right) \boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}$ is the sample covariance matrix of the residuals when regressing $\boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}$ on $\boldsymbol{X}_M$.
%That is, $\rho(\bs X)$ measures how well one can approximate the $\boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}$ with $\boldsymbol{X}_M$.
In an orthonormal case where $\bs X^{\top}\bs X=n\I_p$, then $\rho(\bs X)=1$.  

\begin{lem}\label{lem:boundrho}
 For any $M\in\M$, let $Q_S=S \cup M$. Then the non-centrality parameter satisfies  \eqref{eq:muQM}
    $\mu_{Q_SM}\; \geq \;n\,\rho(\bs X)\,\sum_{j=1}^b|S_j\setminus M_j|\,{\beta_{\min,\,j}^*}^2$.
\end{lem}
If $M\neq S$ is such that $M\not\supset S$ and $M\not\subset S$, simultaneously large enough $\kappa_j$ and $\mu_{Q_S M}$ guarantee that $C(S)-C(M)$ is also large, and that $NC(M)$ vanishes.


\subsection{Sufficient conditions for consistency with block $\ell_0$ penalties}
%\subsection{Selection based on block $\ell_0$ penalty}
\label{ssec:regression_mainresult}

We now state two conditions that are sufficient for asymptotically recovering $S$. 
Building on our previous discussion, we require the block penalties $\kappa_j$ to be large enough,  
and a betamin condition. \\[5pt] 
\hypertarget{lab:A6}{(A6)} \quad For each block $j$, there exists $f_j\to \infty$ (as $n\to \infty$) such that for every sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{equation*}
    \kappa_j \;=\;\ln(p_j-s_j) + f_j
\end{equation*} 
\hypertarget{lab:A7}{(A7)} \quad For each block $j$, there exists $g_j\to \infty$ such that for every sufficiently large $n$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \sqrt{\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{6}}{\beta_{\min,j}^*} \,- \,\sqrt{\kappa_j} \;=\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + g_j .
    \end{equation*}
where $\gamma:=\tfrac12(1+\max_j\ln(p_j-s_j)/\kappa_j)\in (\tfrac12,1)$.\\[5pt] 
These assumptions are similar to Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A4}{A4}--\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} formulated for the sequence model.
 By Proposition~\ref{prop:isthresh}, in the orthonormal 
 case where $\bs X^{\top}\bs X=n\I_p$, setting block penalties $\kappa_j$ 
 is equivalent to hard-thresholding with block thresholds $\tau_j=\sqrt{2 \kappa_j/n}$.
 Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A4}{A4}--\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} can then be translated into assumptions on $\kappa_j$, taking $\rho(\bs X)=1$. In that case, Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} is of the same order as Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A4}{A4}, up to a term $f_j$ that can grow at an arbitrarily slow rate. %An oracle choice for $f_j$ is presented in Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel}. 
 Further, if one sets $f_j$ such that $\ln(p_j-s_j)= O(f_j)$ then $1-\gamma > 0$, and then Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} essentially requires $n \rho(\bs X) \beta_{\min,j}^*$ to be larger than $\sqrt{\kappa_j} + \sqrt{\ln(s_j)}$ (up to constants and a term growing at an arbitrarily slow rate), analogously to Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5}.
% A natural choice stemming from a Bayesian perspective discussed in Section~\ref{sec:informed_l0}, is $f_j=0.5\ln(n)$. %for all $j$. 
%\color{blue} Then, if $p= O(n^k)$ for some $k>0$ we have that $1-\gamma>0$. \color{black}
% The betamin Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} is the same as \hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} up to, again, a term $g_j$ increasing at arbitrarily slow rate, and the factor $\sqrt{(1-\gamma)/3}$. \david{In \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} I see $(1-\gamma)/6$ not $(1-\gamma)/3$.} This factor is smaller than $1/2$ and depends on the choice of $f_j$'s in \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6}. \david{"This factor..." is confusing, better state the factor.} It is bounded away from 0 as long as $f_j= \Omega(\ln(p_j-s_j))$ for all $j$. \david{Can we instead say $\ln(p_j-s_j)=O(f_j)$ in the previous sentence? And in the next sentence, say instead: For example, if $f_j=0.5 \ln(n)$ then $(1-\gamma)>0$ as long as $p=O(n^k)$.}
% This is the case for the earlier natural choice \david{"earlier natural choice is confusing", specify} whenever $n^k=\Omega(p)$ for some $k>0$. \paul{essentially NOT ultra high dimension} \david{Can we just say $p=O(n^k)$?} When $f_j= o(\ln(p_j-s_j))$ for some $j$, \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} requires $\sqrt{\kappa_j}+\sqrt{\ln(s_j)} = o(n\rho(\bs X)\beta_{min,j})$.
%\david{Earlier we said that $f_j$ can grow at an arbitrarily slow rate, but we now see that $f_j$ has to be at least of order $p_j-s_j$. How about saying: This factor depends on the choice of $f_j$ in \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6}. For example, by default we take $f_j=...$, in which case $\gamma$ is bounded below by ... Perhaps also relate the choice of $(f_j,g_j)$ to the oracle $\kappa_j^*$'s discussed later in the theorem, like "an ideal choice of $(f_j,g_j)$ leads to oracle penalties $\kappa_j^*$ given in Theorem 4.2 below" (and, if it helps, briefly explain how the oracle $(f_j,g_j)$ depend on $s_j,p_j,\beta_{\min}^*$)} \paul{$f_j$ can grow at any rate.  For any growth rate you will discard asymptotically the overfitting models. When $f_j= o(\ln(p_j-s_j))$ for some $j$, the betamin requirement is stronger than $\Theta(\sqrt{\kappa_j} +\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + g_j)$. To see it, the betamin requirement is essentially: $\sqrt{\frac{ n\rho(\bs X)}{2}}{\beta_{\min,j}^*}  \;=2(\max_j\frac{\ln(p_j-s_j)}{f_j}+1)(\sqrt{\kappa_j} +\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)}) + g_j$. It is not very intuitive to have such change in betamin condition i know. It is linked to a technicality of the proof. I have tried hard to get rid of it but could not. I have explained more.}
%\david{I get confused by first seeing $f_j=\Omega(\ln(p_j-s_j))$ and later $f_j=o(\ln(p_j-s_j))$.}
Finally, we remark that in our proof one could take a betamin condition that is slightly less strict than Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7}, but we present Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} here to facilitate comparison to Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} in the sequence model.  


We can now state our main theorem on the strong variable selection consistency of $\hat{S}^{b}$. The result holds for either fixed or diverging $p_j-s_j$ and $s_j$. %\color{green}holding fixed or diverging numbers of signals:\color{black}
\begin{thm}\label{theo:suffcondlinearmodel}

Under Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}, \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and, \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7}, we have
$$\lim_{n \to \infty} \sum_{M \in \M\setminus \{S\}}\E\left(NC(M)\right)= 0 \qquad \mbox{and} \qquad \lim_{n \to \infty} P(\hat{S}^b = S)= 1.$$
\end{thm}


Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} considers a linear penalty across blocks
$\sum_{j=1}^b \kappa_j|M_j|$. This linearity assumption can be relaxed, and in section S5 of the Supplement we give consistency results for nonlinear penalties. 
%Those apply for example to the EBIC penalty \citep{EBIC} and improve on its sufficient betamin conditions for selection consistency given in \cite{ebicdiverging}. 

Prior results support the tightness of the conditions of Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel}. Firstly, when translated to the orthonormal setting Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} are similar to Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A4}{A4}--\hyperlink{lab:A5}{A5} where shown to be necessary or near-necessary in Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth}. Secondly, when applied to $\hat{S}$ ($\hat{S}^b$ with $b=1$), Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} shows that, for a wide range of asymptotic regimes, standard $\ell_0$ penalties achieve support recovery under the same conditions as an optimal selector that already knows $s$ analyzed in \cite{infotheowainwright}. In some regimes, our sufficient assumptions are even weaker than those for the optimal selector in \cite{infotheowainwright}. In Supplement S7, we precisely discuss the tightness of Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7}, also showing they match our necessary conditions of Section~\ref{ssec:neccond_linreg} in a wide range of regimes. Moreover, unlike the results in \cite{infotheowainwright}, Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} does not make distributional assumptions on $\bs X$ and shows consistency in a $L_1$ sense.

From the proof of Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel}, we can bound the convergence rate of $P(\hat S^{b}\neq S)$, given in Theorem~\ref{prop:fullrecovreg}. We also give oracle block penalties that approximately optimize the bound, and provide the resulting oracle rate of convergence. 
In the statement, $\delta<1$ and $r>1$ should be understood as being arbitrarily close to 1. 
%\david{The theorem below is really a corollary no?}\paul{It is not really a corollary. It is actually the other way around: in the proof of Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} I prove the first bound below and the consistency follows from that bound}
\begin{thm}\label{prop:fullrecovreg}
Assume \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}, \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6}, \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7}. %\david{Don't we assume \hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2} as in Theorem 4.5?}\paul{No, you don't need \hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2} to state the bound, it is valid for all regimes of $p$} 
Then, for all sufficiently large $n$ and any $\delta \in (0,1)$ and $r>1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rateconvreg}
 P(\hat{S}^{b} \neq S)\;\leq\; 6(2^{2b}-2b)r\,\sum_{j=1}^b\,\, e^{-\tfrac{\delta}{2}\big[\kappa_j-\ln(p_j-s_j)\big]} + 
    e^{-\tfrac{\delta}{2}\big[(\sqrt{\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{6}}{\beta_{\min,j}^*} - \sqrt{\kappa_j})^2 -\ln(s_j) \big]}.
\end{equation}
%where $r_b\to 1$ as $n\to \infty$.
Moreover, suppose that for all $j=1,\ldots,b$ $\lim_{n\to \infty}\big(\sqrt{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)/3}\;\beta_{\min,j}^*\big)/\big(\sqrt{2\ln(p_j-s_j)} + \sqrt{2\ln(s_j)}\big) >1$ and the $\kappa_j$ are set at the oracle values
	\begin{equation} \label{eq:oraclepenreg}
\sqrt{\kappa^*_j\,} \;=\; \frac12\sqrt{\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{6}}{\beta_{\min,j}^*}+\frac12\sqrt{\frac{6}{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}}\frac{1}{\beta_{\min,j}^*}\big(\ln({p_j-s_j})-\ln({s_j})\big).
\end{equation}
Then Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} hold. Moreover, if Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1} holds too, then
% Then for all sufficiently large $n$, Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} hold and, if Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1} holds too, 
%for any $\delta \in (0,1)$ and $r>1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:oraclerateconvreg}
    P(\hat{S}^{b} \neq S) \; \leq \; 12(2^{2b}-2b)r \sum_{j=1}^b e^{-\tfrac{\delta}{2}\big[\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{24}{\beta_{\min,j}^*}^2-\ln \max\{p_j-s_j,s_j\}\big]}.
\end{equation}
%where $r_b\to 1$ as $n\to\infty$.
\end{thm}
In the orthonormal setting, using the equivalence $\tau_j=\sqrt{2 \kappa_j/n}$, the bounds in \eqref{eq:rateconvreg} and \eqref{eq:oraclepenreg} nearly recover the tight bounds in \eqref{eq:rateconvgsm} and \eqref{eq:optimtauj} for the sequence model. 

\subsection{Necessary assumptions for consistency with block $\ell_0$ penalties}\label{ssec:neccond_linreg}

We derive assumptions on $\kappa_j$ and $\beta_{min,j}$ that are necessary for consistent variable selection with $\hat{S}^b$. %Recall that the strength of the block penalties $\kappa_j$ drives the vanishing of the pseudo-posterior probabilities of over-fitted models.
%The block penalties need then to be large enough for the pseudo-posterior probabilities of even the least penalized over-fitted models to vanish. 
For every $j=1,\ldots,b$, denote by $O_j$ the set of models that over-fit by only one variable from block $B_j$:
$$O_j \:=\,\{M\in \M\;|\; M=S\cup\{i\} \text{ where } i\in B_j\setminus S_j\}.$$
The asymptotic recovery of $S$ implies that, $\max_{M\in O_j}NC(M)/NC(S)<1$ with probability going to 1 as $n$ grows, for every $j=1,\ldots,b$. For every $M \in O_j$ the ratio $NC(M)/NC(S)$ grows with $L_{MS}$
%$M_k\in O_j$, the ratio $NC(M_k)/NC(S)$ grows with $L_{M_kS}$ 
which, by Lemma~\ref{lemma:s7}, is $\chi^2_1$ distributed (note that $\bs \beta_{Q_S\setminus S}^*=\bs \beta_{M\setminus S}^*=0$). Then, for every $M \in O_j$, there exists $Z_M \sim N(0,1)$ such that $L_{MS}=Z_{M}^2$.
%$M_k\in O_j$, $Z_{M_k} \sim N(0,1)$ such that $L_{M_kS}=Z_{M_k}^2$. 
%A necessary assumption on $\kappa_j$ follows from properties of the maximum of correlated Gaussian variables.
Let
%Denote the smallest eigenvalue of their correlation matrix:
\begin{align}
    &\underline{\lambda}_j\;:=\;\lambda_{min}(C^j)\;\;\text{where} \;\;C^j_{k,l}=\operatorname{corr}\big(Z_{M_k},Z_{M_l}\big),\;\;\forall M_k,M_l \in O_j
    \nonumber \\
    &\underline{\lambda}\;:=\;\lambda_{min}(C)\;\;\text{where} \;\;C_{k,l}=\operatorname{corr}\big(Z_{M_k},Z_{M_l}\big),\;\;\forall M_k,M_l \in \cup_{j=1}^b O_j.
    \nonumber
\end{align}
%\paul{note it is not the correlation matrix of the $\sqrt{L_{MS}}=|Z_M|$. This result is based on \cite{hartigan} that gives a lower bound for maximum of correlated Gaussians and not maximum of absolute value of correlated Gaussians. The lower bound is probably the same but proving it would require additional lemmas.}
%We denote $\underline{\lambda}$, the smallest eigenvalue of the correlation matrix of the $Z_{M_k}$, $M_k\in\cup_{j=1}^b O_j$.
That is, small $\underline{\lambda}_j$ indicates that truly inactive variables in block $j$ are highly correlated with each other, whereas $\underline{\lambda}_j=1$ that they are uncorrelated. Proposition~\ref{prop:nectypeIreg} below describes how $\underline{\lambda}_j$ relates to a necessary condition for consistency.
In Section \ref{ssec:regression_gains} we discuss how $\underline{\lambda}$ describes settings where $\hat S$ is not consistent but $\hat S^b$ is. 
%A lower bound on the maximum of correlated Gaussians in \cite{hartigan} yields the following result.
\begin{prop}\label{prop:nectypeIreg}
Assume \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}-\hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2}.
     If for some $j=1,\ldots,b$,  $\lim_{n\to\infty}\kappa_j/(\underline{\lambda}_j^2\ln(p_j-s_j)) <1$, then  %under Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1} and \hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2}, 
     $$\lim_{n \to \infty} P\Big(\max_{M\in O_j}\frac{NC(M)}{NC(S)}<1\Big)= 0 \quad\text{and}\quad \lim_{n \to \infty} P(\hat{S}^b=S)= 0.$$
\end{prop}



Proposition~\ref{prop:nectypeIreg} shows that 
a necessary assumption to recover $S$ asymptotically
is 
\begin{equation}\label{cond:necIallblockpen}
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\kappa_j}{\underline{\lambda}_j^2\ln(p_j-s_j)}\;\geq\;1\qquad\mbox{for all }j=1,\ldots,b.
\end{equation}
%When the inactive variables are orthogonal, for every $j=1,\ldots,b$ and $M\in O_j$, the $Z_M$ are independent and $\underline{\lambda}_j=1$. Then
When $\underline{\lambda}_j=1$ as in the orthonormal case, \eqref{cond:necIallblockpen} recovers %Under orthogonal design, \eqref{cond:necIallblockpen} then recovers 
the necessary condition shown in Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecblockthresh} (ii) for the sequence model. More generally we have $\underline{\lambda}_j \in [0,1]$, and then \eqref{cond:necIallblockpen} is actually a milder condition than that shown in Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecblockthresh} (ii). That is, $\underline{\lambda}_j=1$ corresponds to the worst case, in terms of controlling false positives.

Consider now an under-fitted model $M\subset S$. %The recovery of $S$ implies that with probability going to 1 as $n$ grows, $NC(M)/NC(S)<1$. 
By definition the ratio $NC(M)/NC(S)$ %By definition of $NC$ the ratio 
grows with block penalties $\kappa_j$ and shrinks with $L_{SM}$, which is distributed $\chi^2_{|S\setminus M|}(\mu_{SM})$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:s7}.
For the ratio to be small, $\mu_{SM}$ must grow
%Small values of the ratio are attained only if $\mu_{SM}$ grows 
fast enough compared to $\kappa_j$. Lemma~\ref{lem:upperboundnoncentral} shows that $\mu_{SM}$ is bounded by the largest active signals in $S$ that is not in $M$.
\begin{lem}\label{lem:upperboundnoncentral}
    For any $T\subseteq S$, $\mu_{ST} \leq n\,\bar{\lambda}\,\sum_{j=1}^b|S_j\setminus T_j|\,\max_{i\in S_j\setminus T_j}{\beta_{i}^*}^2$  where $\bar{\lambda}\,:=\,\lambda_{\max}\Big(n^{-1} \bs X_{S}^\top\bs X_{S}\Big)$
\end{lem}

It follows that a necessary condition is that the active $\beta_i^*$ that are missing in any underfitted $M$ are not too small. We next formally define what we mean by small and large signals, and also define a subset of intermediate signals 
%introduce a formal distinction between small and large signals in $S$. We also introduce a subset of signals that are not small or large 
that will be used in Section~\ref{ssec:nobetaminhyp}.  For fixed penalties $\kappa_j$'s, and for %and every $j=1,\ldots,b$, 
$\gamma$ and $g_j$ as defined in Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7}, let
\begin{eqnarray}\label{eq:defSes}
    &S_j^{S}(\kappa)\;&:=\;\Bigg\{\beta_i^* \in S_j \,\Big|\,\sqrt{n\bar{\lambda}}|\beta_{i}^*|=o\big(\sqrt{\kappa_j}\big) \Bigg\}\nonumber\\
    &S_j^{L}(\kappa)\;&:=\;\Bigg\{\beta_i^* \in S_j \,\Big|\,\sqrt{\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{6}}|\beta_{i}^*| \,- \,\sqrt{\kappa_j} \;=\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + g_j\Bigg\}\label{eq:defslsi}\\
    &S_j^{I}(\kappa)\;&:=\;S_j \setminus \big(S_j^{L}(\kappa) \cup S_j^{S}(\kappa)\big).
    \nonumber 
\end{eqnarray} \\
The subset $S_j^{S}(\kappa)$ gathers signals in $S_j$ that are small with respect to the penalty $\kappa_j$, $S_j^{L}(\kappa)$ those that are large in that they satisfy Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7}, and $S_j^{I}(\kappa)$ those that are neither large nor small. 
Proposition~\ref{prop:nectypeIIreg} below says that if the set of small signals $S^{S}(\kappa)=\cup_{j=1}^b S_j^{S}(\kappa)$ is not empty, then consistent model recovery is not possible.
%Take $M=S\setminus S^{S}(\kappa)$ where $S^{S}(\kappa)=\cup_{j=1}^b S_j^{S}(\kappa)$. If $S^{S}(\kappa)\neq \emptyset$, the missing active signals in $M$ and thus $\mu_{SM}$ are all small compared to the $\kappa_j$'s. \david{Should it be that "some active signals are small"? (rather than all)} 
%We then establish the following result.
%Using the properties of the noncental $\chi^2$ distribution, we then establish the following convergence of the probabilities of $(NC(M)/NC(S)<1)$ and thus of $(\hat{S}^{b}=S)$.

\begin{prop}\label{prop:nectypeIIreg}
    If $S^{S}(\kappa)\neq\emptyset$ and for all $j=1,\ldots,b$, $\kappa_j\to \infty$, then, under Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1},
    $$\lim_{n \to \infty} P\Big(\frac{NC(S\setminus S^{S}(\kappa))}{NC(S)}<1\Big)= 0 \quad\text{and}\quad \lim_{n \to \infty} P(\hat{S}^b=S)= 0.$$
\end{prop}
%Property~\ref{prop:nectypeIIreg} shows that for any increasing penalty, when $S$ contains signals that are too small with respect to that penalty, the smaller model $S\setminus S^{S}(\kappa)$ is favored at the expense of $S$ and recovery fails. 
It follows that a necessary assumption for variable selection consistency is
\begin{equation}\label{cond:necIIallblockpen}
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt{n\bar{\lambda}}\beta_{\min,j}^*}{\sqrt{\kappa_j}}\;>\;0\qquad\mbox{for all }j=1,\ldots,b.
\end{equation}
When active variables are orthonormal, %orthogonal and normalized, 
that is $\bs X_S^{\top}\bs X_S = nI$, then $\bar{\lambda}=1$. 
Assumption~\eqref{cond:necIIallblockpen} is then milder and less tight than the corresponding necessary assumption on signal strength shown in Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecblockthresh} (iv)  in the sequence model. 

An immediate consequence of Proposition~\ref{prop:nectypeIreg} and Proposition~\ref{prop:nectypeIIreg} is the next result.
\begin{cor}\label{cor:necrecovreg}
    If for some $j\in\{1,\ldots,b\}$   $\lim_{n\to\infty}\sqrt{n\bar{\lambda}}\beta_{\min,j}^*/\big(\underline{\lambda}_j\sqrt{\ln(p_j-s_j)}\big) = 0$, then, under Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1} and \hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2},  $\lim_{n \to \infty} P(\hat{S}^b=S) < 1$.
\end{cor}
Corollary~\ref{cor:necrecovreg} yields a necessary assumption for consistency
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betaminnecreg}
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt{n\bar{\lambda}}\beta_{\min,j}^*}{\underline{\lambda}_j\sqrt{\ln(p_j-s_j)}} > 0\qquad\mbox{for all }j=1,\ldots,b.
\end{equation}
%In an orthonormal design where $\bs X^{\top} \bs X=nI$, %for all $j=1,\ldots,b$, 
%then $\underline{\lambda}_j=1$ and $\bar{\lambda}=1$. 
In the particular case of orthonormality and diverging $s$, the necessary assumption \eqref{eq:betamin} given for the sequence model is then stricter and tighter than \eqref{eq:betaminnecreg}. Assumption \eqref{eq:betaminnecreg} is however a more general necessary betamin condition that applies in all correlated, orthonormal, low and high dimensional settings, for $s$ fixed or diverging. In Supplement S7, we contrast necessary condition~\eqref{eq:betaminnecreg} with the sufficient conditions, assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7}, for standard selector $\hat{S}$. Our analysis shows that they imply the same scaling of $(n,p)$ in a wide range of regimes, confirming their tightness. %\paul{I am happy about this result because I believe there was no such tight necessary assumption for variable selection consistency in regression in the literature. The only ones I found were for LASSO (far less efficient/tight for selection in correlated settings) or compressed sensing}.

\subsection{Benefits of block penalties}
\label{ssec:regression_gains}

We discuss separately the benefits in terms of sufficient conditions for variable selection consistency and those in terms of convergence rate. The results are analogous to those in Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth} for the sequence model. %main message is that benefits discussed in the sequence model in Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth} extend to linear regression. 

\smallskip

\noindent Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6}--\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} give ranges of penalties that are sufficient (but not necessary) for asymptotic support recovery. For simplicity, we restrict our discussion to choices of penalty $\kappa_j$ such that, in Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6}, $\ln(p_j-s_j)=O(f_j)$ for all $j$, so that $\gamma$ is bounded in $(0,1/2)$. For the standard selector $\hat{S}$, the single penalty $\kappa$ is essentially required to satisfy,
for some sequences $f,g\to\infty$ and up to constants
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rangepenex}
  \sqrt{\ln(p-s)}+ f\;\;\leq\;\;\sqrt{\kappa}\;\;\leq\;\;\sqrt{n\rho(\bs X)}\beta^*_{\min}+\sqrt{\ln(s)} + g.	
\end{equation}
For a block selector $\hat{S}^{b}$, the ranges for the $\kappa_j$'s essentially are, for some sequences $f_j,g_j\to\infty$ and up to constants
    \begin{eqnarray}\label{eq:rangeblockpenex}
      \sqrt{\ln(p_j-s_j)}+f_j\;\;&\leq\;\;\sqrt{\kappa_j}\;\;\leq&\;\;\sqrt{n\rho(\bs X)}\beta^*_{\min,j}-\sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + g_j.
      %
%  \sqrt{\ln(p_1-s_1)}\;\;&<\;\;\sqrt{\kappa_1}\;\;<&\;\;\sqrt{n\rho(\bs X)}\beta^*_{\min,1}-\sqrt{\ln(s_1)}\nonumber\\
%  &\;\;\vdots \\
%  \sqrt{\ln(p_b-s_b)}\;\;&<\;\;\sqrt{\kappa_b}\;\;<&\;\;\sqrt{n\rho(\bs X)}\beta^*_{\min}-\sqrt{\ln(s_b)} \nonumber.       
    \end{eqnarray}
Akin to the sequence model, if there exist two blocks such that the ranges in \eqref{eq:rangeblockpenex} do not overlap, then a constant $\kappa$ cannot satisfy \eqref{eq:rangeblockpenex} for both blocks and consistent selection may not be possible.
%by definition, the ranges for $\hat S^b$ are wider than that for $\hat S$. 
That is, sufficient conditions for variable selection consistency are milder with block penalties. Although not discussed here for brevity, block penalties also lead to similar improvements on the necessary conditions discussed in Section \ref{ssec:neccond_linreg}, relative to those standard $\ell_0$ penalties.
Corollary~\ref{cor:selconstimpossiblereg} gives conditions under which consistent selection is possible with $\hat S^b$ but not with $\hat S$.
\begin{cor}\label{cor:selconstimpossiblereg}
Assume \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}, \hyperlink{lab:A2}{A2}, \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6}, \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7}. If
$\lim_{n\to\infty}\big(\sqrt{n\bar{\lambda}}\beta_{\min}^*\big)/\big(\underline{\lambda}\sqrt{\ln(p-s)}\big) = 0$
then 
$P(\hat{S} = S)\not \to 1$ and $P(\hat{S}^{b} = S)\to 1$.
\end{cor}

Observe that \eqref{eq:rangepenex} and \eqref{eq:rangeblockpenex} are analogous to \eqref{eq:rangethreshex} and \eqref{eq:rangeblockthreshex} for the sequence model, up to rescaling by $\sqrt{2/n}$, the factor $\rho(\bs X)$ and sequences $(f_j,g_j)$ growing arbitrarily slowly. The gains in terms of valid thresholds for consistency discussed in the examples of Section \ref{sec:examples} remain applicable %are essentially the same for 
to linear regression. %The main difference lies in that $\rho(\bs X)$ may be smaller than 1 making the range for $\hat S$ narrower and $\hat S^b$ even more beneficial. 

Let $\underline{\beta}^{*,b}_{\min,reg}$ and $\underline{\beta}^{*}_{\min,reg}$ be the smallest signal recoverable by $\hat{S}^b$ and $\hat{S}$ respectively.
%Consider the smallest signal recoverable by $\hat{S}^b$ and $\hat{S}$ while still being variable selection consistent that we denote $\underline{\beta}^{*,b}_{\min,reg}$ and $\underline{\beta}^{*}_{\min,reg}$. 
Assuming $\beta_{min}^*$ is in block $b$, Assumptions~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6}-\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} essentially require that $\underline{\beta}^{*,b}_{\min,reg}$ and $\underline{\beta}^{*}_{\min,reg}$ satisfy for some sequences $g,h\to\infty$ and up to constants
%they are essentially required by \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} to satisfy, up to bounded factors,
\begin{eqnarray*}
    \underline{\beta}^{*,b}_{\min,reg}&\geq& \sqrt{\frac{\ln(p_b-s_b)}{ n\rho(\bs X)}} + \sqrt{\frac{\ln(s_b)}{ n\rho(\bs X)}} + g,\quad\text{and}  \\
    \underline{\beta}^{*}_{\min,reg}&\geq& \sqrt{\frac{\ln(p-s)}{ n\rho(\bs X)}} + \sqrt{\frac{\ln(s)}{ n\rho(\bs X)}}  + h.
\end{eqnarray*}
These lower bounds are the same as \eqref{eq:betaminbardef} for the sequence model, up to rescaling by $\sqrt{2}$, a factor $\rho(\bs X)$ and $g$ and $h$ which can grow arbitrarily slowly with $n$. Hence, the discussion and examples of the benefits in the smallest recoverable signals in Section~\ref{sec:examples} extend to linear regression.

Let ${OR}^b_{reg}$ be the oracle convergence rate for $\hat{S}^{b}$ in Theorem~\ref{prop:fullrecovreg}, and ${OR}_{reg}$ that for $\hat{S}$. Then we have
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{{OR}^b_{reg}}{{OR}_{reg}}=(2^{2b-1}-b)\sum_{j=1}^be^{-\frac{\delta}{2}\left[\frac{ n\rho(\bs X)}{24}\left((1-\gamma){\beta^*_{\min,j}}^2-(1-\gamma'){\beta_{\min}^*}^2\right)+\ln \max\{p-s,s\}-\ln \max\{p_j-s_j,s_j\}\right]},
\end{equation*}
where $\gamma=\tfrac12(1+\max_j\ln(p_j-s_j)/\kappa_j^*)$ and $\gamma'=\tfrac12(1+\ln(p-s)/\kappa^*)$. The ratio above is essentially the same as \eqref{eq:ratioCR} for the sequence model, up to certain factors.  Specifically, $\delta$, $(1-\gamma)$ and $(1-\gamma')$ are close to 1, whereas $ \rho(\bs X)/24$ slows the convergence rate gains but does not alter the essence of the ratio. % as long as $\rho(\bs X)$ remains bounded above 0. 
The factor $2^{2b-1}$ highlights however a potential limitation: guarantees of gains with block $\ell_0$ penalties deteriorate when one considers a large number of blocks $b$.
We remark that such deterioration may be a consequence of our proof strategy, rather than an inherent limitation of block penalties. Studying cases with %large $b$, including 
$b \to \infty$ is left as future research.

\subsection{Convergence with no betamin condition}\label{ssec:nobetaminhyp}

We now derive a convergence result for pseudo-posterior probabilities $NC(M)$, $M\in\mathcal{M}$ under no assumption on the minimal signal strength. The result generalizes Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} and is key to the proofs of Section~\ref{sec:informed_l0}.

%In \eqref{eq:defslsi}, we defined, for a choice of penalties $\kappa_j$, the set of large and intermediate signals. 
Let $\T(\kappa)$ be the set of models that contain all large signals signals in $S^{L}(\kappa)=\cup_{j=1}^b S_j^{L}(\kappa)$, and neither truly inactive parameters in $V\setminus S$ nor small signals in $S^S(\kappa)=\cup_{j=1}^b S^{S}_j(\kappa)$. That is,
\begin{equation} \label{eq:Tkappa}
   \T(\kappa) \;:=\;\Big\{M \in \M \,\big|\, M\,=\, S^L(\kappa)\,\cup\,R,\; R\in \mathcal{P}\big(S^{I}(\kappa)\big)  \Big\},
\end{equation}
where for a set $A$, $ \mathcal{P}(A)$ denotes the power set of $A$.
Theorem~\ref{thm:convtoT} below shows,
%Following a strategy similar to that of the proof of Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel}, we demonstrate, 
assuming only sufficiently large penalties as given in Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6},  that posterior model pseudo-probabilities concentrate on $\T(\kappa)$.
%the vanishing of the sum of pseudo-probabilities of all models but those in $\T(\kappa)$. 
%The main difference with respect to the proof of Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} is that, for any $M\not\in \T(\kappa)$, we bound $\E(NC(M))$ by a function of $C(M)-C(T)$ for a well chosen model $T$ in $\T(\kappa)$. 
\begin{thm}\label{thm:convtoT}
    Assume \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}, \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6}, $|S^{I}(\kappa)|=O(1)$ and $|S^{S}_j(\kappa)|=O(p_j-s_j)$ for every $j=1,\ldots,b$. Then
    $$\lim_{n \to \infty} \sum_{M \in \M\setminus \T(\kappa)} \E\left(NC(M)\right)= 0 \qquad \mbox{and} \qquad
    \lim_{n \to \infty} P(\hat{S}^b \in \T(\kappa))= 1.$$
\end{thm}
%Theorem~\ref{thm:convtoT} shows that, asymptotically, with only assuming
That is, if one sets
sufficiently large penalties, all inactive and all small active signals are discarded. On the other hand, all large signals (relative to the specified penalties) are retained. Intermediate signals in $S^{I}(\kappa)$ may or may not be retained. Assumption $|S^{I}(\kappa)|=O(1)$ was made for simplicity, in fact $|S^{I}(\kappa)|$ can be allowed to increase moderately. 

\section{Data analysis with block $\ell_0$ penalization}\label{sec:informed_l0}

In Sections~\ref{sec:properties_orth} and~\ref{sec:properties_legression}, we derived properties for block penalties where one sets the penalties to oracle values.
We now
propose two data analysis methods anchored in an empirical Bayes perspective
that do not require oracle values, and adapt to the unknown sparsity in the data-generating truth.
The main idea is that, by using a BIC approximation to the marginal likelihood, one may estimate the proportion of truly active variables in each block by the average posterior inclusion probabilities in that block. This provides
%A Bayesian approach leads to a natural estimator of the number of truly active variables in each block and thus, by using empirical Bayes, 
a straightforward way to adapt penalties to sparsity in each block. Relying on the BIC approximation also allows the use of fast Bayesian computational methods that overcome the intractability of $\ell_0$ penalties. 
We remark that, despite their Bayesian motivation, the methods are fully data-dependent and do not require any prior distribution.

\subsection{An estimator of sparsity}\label{ssec:est.sparsity}

In a Bayesian framework for \eqref{eq:linearmodel} the model $M=(m_1,\ldots,m_p)$ is a vector of variable inclusion indicators $m_i=I(\beta_i \neq 0)$. Let $|M|=\sum_{j=1}^p m_j$. Consider a joint prior on parameters and models 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bayesianframework}
    p(\boldsymbol{\beta},M \mid \boldsymbol{\theta})\;=\;p( \boldsymbol{\beta} \mid  M) p(M \mid \boldsymbol{\theta})
\end{equation}
where $p( \boldsymbol{\beta} \mid  M)$ is a prior on regression coefficients given the model, and 
$p(M \mid \boldsymbol{\theta})$ the prior probability of model $M$. The latter depends on hyperparameters $\bs \theta$ giving the prior inclusion probabilities in each block.
Specifically, assume that variable inclusions are independent a priori,
with constant inclusion probability $\theta^{(j)}$ within each block $j$. Then
\begin{eqnarray} \label{eq:block_modelprior}
p( M \mid \boldsymbol{\theta}) \propto
        \prod_{i=1}^p \operatorname{Bern}\left(m_i ; \theta_i\right) I(M \in \M),
\end{eqnarray}
where $\forall \, i\in B_j, \: \theta_i=\theta^{(j)}$ and $\bs \theta=(\theta^{(1)},\ldots,\theta^{(b)})$.

Posterior model probabilities are $p(M \mid \bs y, \bs \theta) \propto p(\bs y \mid M) p(M \mid \bs \theta)$, where $p(\bs y \mid M)$ is the so-called marginal likelihood of model $M$. The BIC approximation \citep{BIC} to $p( \bs y  \mid M)$ gives
%\begin{eqnarray*}
% p( \bs y  \mid M)\;\approx\; p(\bs y \mid \tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)})n^{-|M|/2}
%\end{eqnarray*}
%Hence
%It follows that the posterior model probabilities satisfy
\begin{eqnarray*}
\ln p(M \mid\bs y, \bs \theta) \;\approx\; \ln p(\bs y \mid \tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)}) - \frac{|M|}{2}\ln(n) + \ln p(M\mid\bs \theta) + c_M,
\end{eqnarray*}
for a wide family of priors $p(\bs \beta \mid M)$, where $\tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)}$ is the MLE under model $M$, and $c_M$ a constant that may depend on $M$.
In particular, when $p(\bs \beta \mid M)$ is Zellner's unit information prior \citep{zellner1986assessing}, then $c_M$ does not depend on $M$ and the approximation is exact by replacing $\tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)}= (\bs X_M^\top \bs X_M + n^{-1} I)^{-1} \bs X_M^\top \bs y$.
Neglecting the constant $c_M$, simple algebra shows that 
the block $\ell_0$ penalty selector discussed in Section \ref{sec:properties_legression} approximately maximizes $p(M \mid \bs y, \bs \theta)$. %for some $p(M \mid \bs \theta)$. 
Specifically, take the normalized criterion $NC(M)$ in \eqref{eq:CM} to be proportional to
\begin{align}
p(\bs y \mid \tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)})  
\prod_{j=1}^b \left( n^{\frac{1}{2}}(1/\theta^{(j)} - 1) \right)^{-|M_j|},
    \nonumber
\end{align}
which corresponds to taking the block penalties  
%Then $\ln p(M \mid \bs \theta)$ defines the block penalties studied in Section \ref{sec:properties_legression}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:blockpenpriorinclu}
    \kappa_j=\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(n)+\ln\big(1/{\theta^{(j)}} - 1\big).
\end{equation}

In summary, one may think of $NC(M) \approx P(M \mid \bs y,\bs \theta)$, where the $\kappa_j$'s are a suitable function of $\theta^{(j)}$. %, the normalized score in Section \ref{sec:properties_legression}.
This connection motivates estimating the number of truly active variables in block $j$ by %for any block $\ell_0$ procedure:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shat}
    \hat{s}_j \,:=\, \sum_{i\in B_j}\,\,\sum_{M\in \M | i \in M}NC(M)
%\end{equation}
%The normalized score $NC(M)$ can be seen as the posterior probability for model $M$ in Bayesian framework \eqref{eq:bayesianframework}. 
%To be clear, replacing $NC(M)$ by $P(M \mid \bs y,\bs \theta)$ in \eqref{eq:shat} gives
%\begin{equation*}
\approx    \sum_{i\in B_j}\,\,\sum_{M\in \M | i \in M}P(M\mid \bs y,\bs \theta) = \sum_{i\in B_j}P(\beta_i \neq 0 \mid \bs y,\bs \theta),
\end{equation}
where the right-hand side is the posterior mean $E(s_j \mid \bs y,\bs \theta)$.
%Hence, $\hat{s}_j$ is the posterior mean of $s_j$ in a setting where one uses the BIC approximation to the marginal likelihood.
One may then set prior inclusion probabilities $\hat{\theta}^{(j)}= \hat{s}_j/p_j$, the estimated proportion of truly active variables in block $j$.
%From a Bayesian perspective, $\hat{s}_j$ is then %the sum of posterior inclusion probabilities and 
%the posterior mean of $s_j$. 
Section S0 of the Supplement discusses how $\hat{\theta}^{(j)}=\hat{s}_j/p_j$ can also be motivated as an approximation to an empirical Bayes estimator maximizing the marginal likelihood of $\bs y$ given $\bs \theta$.

We now show that, besides being well-founded from a Bayesian perspective, $\hat{\theta}^{(j)}=\hat{s}_j/p_j$ %and, as shown below, also 
has attractive frequentist properties under mild assumptions. In \eqref{eq:defslsi} we defined $S^{S}_j(\kappa)$ to be the set of small signals in block $j$ and  $S^{L}_j(\kappa)$ the set of larger signals. In Theorem~\ref{thm:convtoT} we also derived a convergence result on pseudo posterior probabilities that yields the following asymptotic bounds on the frequentist expectation of $\hat{s}_j/p_j$.
\begin{prop}
    \label{prop:shatconsistence}
Assume \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}, \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6}, $|S^{I}(\kappa)|=O(1)$ and $|S^{S}_j(\kappa)|=O(p_j-s_j)$ for every $j=1,\ldots,b$. Then
$$ \,\, \frac{|S^{L}_j(\kappa)|}{p_j}\leq \lim_{n\to\infty}\E\bigg(\frac{\hat{s}_j}{p_j}\bigg)\leq \frac{s_j-|S^{S}_j(\kappa)|}{p_j}\qquad\text{for all }j=1,\ldots,b$$
\end{prop}
An immediate consequence is that, if the betamin condition in Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} also holds, then $\hat{s}_j/p_j \stackrel{L_1}{\longrightarrow} s_j/p_j$
because $S_j=S_j^{L}(\kappa)$ and $S_j^{S}(\kappa)=\emptyset$ for all $j$. The proposition also shows that, when Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} is not met, $\hat{s}_j/p_j$ is asymptotically downward biased by at least $|S^{S}_j(\kappa)|/p_j$, but it is guaranteed to be larger than the proportion of signals satisfying Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7}. 

\subsection{Data-based block selection method} \label{ssec:databased_blockselection}

The oracle block penalties of Section~\ref{sec:properties_legression} have varying strength depending on the unknown $\beta^*_{\min,j}$ and number of active signals in each block $s_j$, and we just saw that the latter can be reliably estimated. We propose a two-step procedure. First, we use a standard (non-block-based) penalty $\kappa^{\circ}$ and estimate the number of active signals $s_j$ in each block $j$ with $\hat{s}_j$. Second, we use the estimated $s_j$ to set block penalties. The variable selection consistency of our procedure follows from the results of Section~\ref{sec:properties_legression} and holds equally for fixed and diverging $p_j-s_j$ and $s_j$. While, in Section~\ref{sec:properties_legression}, the gains in conditions for consistency were driven by how $p_j-s_j$ compared to $p-s$, here it is driven by how $p_j-|S^L_j (\kappa^{\circ})|$ compares to $p-|S^L (\kappa^{\circ})|$. This occurs because  recovery of small signals in Step 1 is not guaranteed.

The procedure has two variants. In the first variant we directly use the approximate empirical Bayes approach where $\theta^{(j)}= \hat{s}_j/p_j$ %of Lemma~\ref{lem:empiricalbayesinterpret} 
to set block penalties in Step 2. 
The second variant is motivated by Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel}. Considering jointly \eqref{eq:blockpenpriorinclu} and Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6}, a natural choice is setting $\theta^{(j)}=(p_j-s_j+1)^{-1}$. This choice yields block penalties that are sufficiently large for Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} to hold, where $f_j$ in Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} takes value $\ln(n)/2$ for all $j$. The second variant approximates this choice.\\

\textbf{Algorithm 1}
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item Set $\kappa_j=\kappa^{\circ}=\ln(p)+\frac{1}{2}\ln(n)$ 
    for $j=1,\ldots,b$. Compute $\hat{s}_j/p_j$ in \eqref{eq:shat} for $j=1,\ldots,b$.

    \item Obtain $\hat{S}^{EB,b}$ solving \eqref{eq:Mhat} with $\kappa_j^{EB}= \ln(p_j/\hat{s}_j -1)+ \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(n)$.
    Alternatively, obtain $\hat{S}^{A,b}$ solving \eqref{eq:Mhat} with
    $\kappa_j^{A} = \ln(p_j-\hat{s}_j)+ \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(n)$.
\end{enumerate}

We refer to $\hat{S}^{EB,b}$ as the block empirical Bayes selector, and to $\hat{S}^{A,b}$ as the block adaptive selector.
Step 1 approximates posterior model probabilities under equal prior inclusion probabilities $\theta^{(j)}=(p+1)^{-1}$ in \eqref{eq:block_modelprior} across blocks, and estimates the proportion of truly active coefficients with $\hat{s}_j/p_j$. Step 1 can then be approximated by any Bayesian computational method for posterior model probabilities. In Section~\ref{sec:simulation} we use an MCMC algorithm. Step 2 selects a model using the block penalties $\kappa_j^{EB}$ induced by $\theta^{(j)}=\hat{s}_j/p_j$,
or alternatively setting $\kappa_j^{A}= \kappa_j^{EB} + \ln(\hat{s}_j)$. Any fast computational method for the exact or approximate resolution of the $\ell_0$ problem may be used for Step 2. 
Under mild assumptions both $\kappa_j^{EB}$ and $\kappa_j^{A}$ lead to variable selection consistency.
The main difference is that in non-sparse settings where $s$ grows faster than $\sqrt{n}$, the penalty $\kappa_j^{EB}$ can be insufficient and there $\kappa_j^{A}$ might be preferred.

Theorem~\ref{theo:empbayesselconsist} shows
the consistency of $\hat{S}^{EB,b}$ under
assumptions:\\[5pt]
\hypertarget{lab:A8}{(A8)}\quad $s=o(\sqrt{n})$\\[5pt]
\hypertarget{lab:A9}{(A9)}\quad For each block $j$, there exists $a_j\to \infty$ such that for every sufficiently large $n$, 
\begin{equation*}\label{cond:suffIIreg2}
        \sqrt{\frac{ (1-\psi)n\rho(\bs X)}{6}}{\beta_{\min,j}^*} \,- \,\sqrt{\ln\bigg(\frac{p_j}{|S^{L}_j(\kappa^{\circ})|}-1\bigg)+\frac12\ln(n)} \;=\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + a_j .
    \end{equation*}
where $\psi=\tfrac12\Big(1+\max_j \ln(p_j-s_j)/\big(\ln(p_j/s_j-1)+\ln(n)/2\big)\Big)$.
%Using Theorem~\ref{thm:convtoT} and Proposition~\ref{prop:shatconsistence}, we show that under \hyperlink{lab:A8}{A8} and \hyperlink{lab:A9}{A9}, the $\kappa^{EB}_j$'s satisfy \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} with probability going to 1 and conclude on the selection consistency  of $\hat{S}^{EB,b}$.

\begin{thm}\label{theo:empbayesselconsist}
    Assume \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}, \hyperlink{lab:A8}{A8}, \hyperlink{lab:A9}{A9}, and $|S^{S}_j(\kappa^{\circ})|=O(p_j-s_j)$ for every $j=1,\ldots,b$. Then
$\lim_{n \to \infty} P(\hat{S}^{EB,b} = S)= 1$.
\end{thm}

Theorem~\ref{theo:informedl0consist} below shows
the consistency of $\hat{S}^{A,b}$. It no longer requires Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A8}{A8}, and Assumption~\hyperlink{lab:A9}{A9} is replaced by the more stringent Assumption A10: \\[5pt]
\hypertarget{lab:A10}{(A10)}\quad For each block $j$, there exists $c_j\to \infty$ such that for  all sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{equation*}
        \sqrt{\frac{ (1-\xi)n\rho(\bs X)}{2}}\beta_{\min,j}^* \,- \,\sqrt{\ln\big(p_j-|S^{L}_j(\kappa^{\circ})|\big)+\frac12\ln(n)} \;=\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + c_j .
    \end{equation*}
where  $\xi=\tfrac12(1+\max_j \ln(p_j-s_j)/(\ln(p_j-s_j)+0.5\ln(n)))$.

\begin{thm}\label{theo:informedl0consist}
    Assume \hyperlink{lab:A1}{A1}, \hyperlink{lab:A10}{A10}, and $|S^{S}_j(\kappa)|=O(p_j-s_j)$ for every $j=1,\ldots,b$, then \\
$\lim_{n \to \infty} P(\hat{S}^{A,b} = S) = 1$.
\end{thm}

\subsection{Benefits of data-based block selection}

By Theorem~\ref{theo:empbayesselconsist}, a standard (non-block-based) empirical Bayes $\hat S^{EB}$ selector that sets in Step 2 a single common penalty $\kappa^{EB}=\ln(p/\hat{s} -1)+ \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(n)$ is variable selection consistent under the betamin assumption:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betaminEB}
        \sqrt{\frac{ (1-\psi')n\rho(\bs X)}{6}}{\beta_{\min}^*} \,- \,\sqrt{\ln\bigg(\frac{p}{|S^{L}(\kappa^{\circ})|}-1\bigg)+\frac12\ln(n)} \;=\; \sqrt{\ln(s)} + a'_j .
    \end{equation}
where $\psi'=\tfrac12\Big(1+ \ln(p-s)/\big(\ln(p/s-1)+\ln(n)/2\big)\Big)$ and $a'_j \to \infty$. We have
\begin{eqnarray*}
&\sqrt{\ln\bigg(\frac{p}{|S^{L}(\kappa^{\circ})|}-1\bigg)+\frac12\ln(n)} + \sqrt{\ln(s)} \geq \sqrt{\ln\bigg(\frac{p_j}{|S^{L}_j(\kappa^{\circ})|}-1\bigg)+\frac12\ln(n)} + \sqrt{\ln(s_j)}
\end{eqnarray*}
and also $\psi > \psi'$. Sufficient conditions for consistency and the smallest signal recoverable are then milder for $\hat S^{EB,b}$ than for $\hat S^{EB}$. %The smaller $p_j/|S^{L}_j(\kappa^{\circ})|$ is compared to $p/|S^{L}(\kappa^{\circ})|$, the milder is \hyperlink{lab:A9}{A9}  compared to \eqref{eq:betaminEB}. % in block $B_j$. 
%$\hat S^{EB,b}$ is then most beneficial when the external information identifies blocks with low proportion of inactive or small signals.

Similarly, a standard (non-block-based) adaptive selector $\hat S^{A}$ setting a common penalty $\kappa^{A}=\ln(p-\hat{s})+ \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(n)$ in Step 2 is consistent under stricter assumptions than $\hat S^{A,b}$. The smallest signal recoverable is also smaller with $\hat S^{A,b}$. %In that case, the gains are driven by the ability of external information to identify blocks with low \emph{numbers} of inactive or small signals.

%Our lack of convergence rates for $\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$ precludes a detailed discussion of their benefits in this aspect. However, we remark that the $\kappa^{EB}_j$ and $\kappa^{A}_j$ are constantly lower than the single $ \kappa^{EB}$ and $\kappa^{A}$ respectively. This is in contrast to the oracle block penalties $\kappa^*_j$ that are larger in blocks with large signals. As a result, $\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$ have higher probability of false positives and lower probability of false negatives than $\hat S^{EB}$ and $\hat S^{A}$ respectively.  $\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$ underperform in settings with large signals and large number of inactive variables and outperform in settings with small signals. We show next with empirical results that the loss in probability of support recovery with $\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$ is minor while the benefit potentially large.  

%\paul{Theorem on rates in Section~\ref{sec:properties_legression} hold when \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} hold. We can only prove \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} hold with probability going to 1. To have a rate for the EB we would need to have a rate at which \hyperlink{lab:A6}{A6} and \hyperlink{lab:A7}{A7} hold}

\section{Numerical illustrations}\label{sec:simulation}

We illustrate the performance of Algorithm 1 on simulated data. We run our proposed method in linear regression under the asymptotic regimes and block sparsity assumptions of Examples 1, 3 and 4 in Table~\ref{tab:examples}. We also consider an additional setting, Example 5, that highlights differences between $\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$. In that example, we set $p=n/2$, $s=3\ln(n)$, and two blocks such that $p_1-s_1=(n-\sqrt{n})/2$, $p_2-s_2=\sqrt{n}/2$ and $s_1 = s_2=3\ln(n)/2$. In Step 1 of Algorithm 1 we take $\kappa^{\circ}=\tfrac12\ln(n)+\ln(p)$. 
To search over models, we rely on 
%We do not perform an exhaustive enumeration and scoring all of the models. We rely instead on 
the connection between $\ell_0$ penalties and Bayesian variable selection and %search over the model space through 
use the MCMC algorithm in function \texttt{bestIC} in R package \texttt{mombf}. Each visited model is 
%The models explored are 
scored with the BIC approximation \eqref{eq:blockpenpriorinclu}. In Step 2 of Algorithm 1, we obtain $\hat S^{EB,b}$, $\hat S^{A,b}$, $\hat S^{EB}$, $\hat S^{A}$ by scoring all the models visited by the MCMC in Step 1.

We simulate data with $n \in \{20,700\}$ and Gaussian covariates with unit variance and all pairwise correlations equal to 0.5.

Figure~\ref{fig:linregslogn} plots the empirical probabilities of correct recovery for $\hat S^{EB,b}$, $\hat S^{A,b}$, $\hat S^{EB}$, $\hat S^{A}$ and the EBIC penalty, for Examples 1, 2, 4 and 5. The probabilities are computed over 100 simulations for each $n$.
In Examples 1 and 3 where blocks are discriminative,
%In the examples with discriminative blocks, 
$\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$ outperform $\hat S^{EB}$, $\hat S^{A}$ and the EBIC, particularly for small $n$. % in the harder conditions when $n$ is small. 
%When $n$ becomes large, $\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$ lose their edge over $\hat S^{EB}$ and $\hat S^{A}$ respectively. This is expected because the penalties in $\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$ are smaller than in $\hat S^{EB}$ and $\hat S^{A}$. When $n$ is large, false negatives are less of an issue and there is no more benefit in smaller penalties. 
%Importantly, the underperformance of $\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$ is null or minor: the probabilities of the block selectors and their standard counterparts converge to same levels in our examples. $\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$ then control the probability of false positives as well as $\hat S^{EB}$ and $\hat S^{A}$ with smaller penalties: they achieve a better balancing of the probabilities of false negatives and false positives. 
In the nondiscriminative block setting, Example 4, $\hat S^{EB,b}$ and $\hat S^{A,b}$ perform very similarly to  $\hat S^{EB}$ and $\hat S^{A}$ respectively. In Examples 1, 3 and 4, the adaptive block selector $\hat S^{A,b}$ outperforms the empirical Bayes selector $\hat S^{EB,b}$ for large $n$. This occurs because $\hat{S}^{A,b}$ uses larger penalties
%is explained by the larger strength of $\hat S^{A,b}$ penalties 
that control better false positives, whereas false negatives are essentially not an issue anymore for such large $n$. In contrast, Example 5 is a setting where smaller penalties are advantageous, because the number of of inactive variables and $\beta_{\min}^*$ are small. In that case,  $\hat S^{EB,b}$ outperforms $\hat S^{A,b}$.

\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}

We studied %provided an understanding of 
how incorporating external information as possible with data integration and transfer learning can facilitate model selection in the sequence model and high-dimensional linear regression. We studied the case where external information partitions
variables %external information helps partition variables 
into blocks %with potentially distinct characteristics 
and introduced corresponding block-based %externally-informed 
$\ell_0$ selectors. % that let the penalty depend on those blocks. 
We showed that an oracle externally-informed selector converges faster and under milder conditions than the standard $\ell_0$ oracle. In particular, it softens the stringent conditions on signal strength. We also provided concrete data analysis methods that incorporate external information to improve variable selection properties without requiring oracle knowledge. Efficient computation is possible for those methods via standard MCMC technique.

A question %that immediately arise 
for future work is how much the assumption of fixed number of blocks $b$ can be relaxed. Our current proof strategies %consistency results 
are robust to moderate increases in the number of blocks but do not work when $b=p$ for example. Also, our setting is motivated by situations where one has a discrete meta-covariate that allows dividing parameters into blocks, e.g. whether a variable refers to patient history or genomic biomarkers. Hence, another natural extension is to consider continuous meta-covariates, e.g. allow the prior inclusion probability of a covariate on its estimated effect in a related disease.

Another interesting research direction is understanding the benefit of external information for parameter estimation and prediction error.
For example, it is possible to obtain estimation error bounds for the sequence model, but the results depend on the chosen estimator (e.g. $\ell_0$, $\ell_1$ or $\ell_2$) and ensuring their tightness requires separate work elsewhere. By focusing on model selection, we obtained results that apply to essentially all penalties / Bayesian methods in the sequence model.


% We also observe that our results bear similarities with conclusions from on related problems. In Section \ref{sec:examples}, we discussed how the benefits of externally-informed selectors depend on how discriminative is the external information. \cite{scarlett2012compressed} similarly showed that support recovery in compressed sensing becomes easier as the entropy of the distribution of inclusion probabilities across blocks shrinks. In hypothesis testing, \cite{genovese2006} demonstrated that the power of their informed weighted testing procedure increases when assignment of weights is positively associated with the null hypotheses being false. We expect analogous results to be achievable in other related problems like structural learning in graphical models or variable selection over partially ordered sets as introduced in \cite{taeb}, which are further interesting future research avenues.

\section{Additional motivation for our estimator of sparsity}\label{suppsec:ebayes} In Section~\ref{ssec:est.sparsity}, we presented our estimator of the proportion of truly active variables in block $j$ as the posterior mean $E(s_j \mid \bs y,\bs \theta)$. A related standard empirical Bayes strategy is to set $\hat{\theta}^{(j)}$ by %The estimator of the proportion of truly active variables $\hat{\theta}^{(j)}= \hat{s}_j/p_j$ can also be justified as an empirical Bayes estimator %procedure. 
%The empirical Bayes estimator of $\bs \theta$ (cf \eqref{eq:block_modelprior}) 
maximizing the marginal likelihood of $\bs y$ given $\bs \theta$, i.e.
\begin{eqnarray}
\hat{\bs \theta}
%\hat{\bs \theta}^{EB}
\,:=\, \arg\max_{\bs \theta} p(\bs y \mid \bs \theta)=
\arg\max_{\bs \theta} \int p(\bs y \mid \bs \beta, M) d P(\bs \beta, M \mid \bs \theta).
\nonumber
\end{eqnarray}
% In the Bayesian framework \eqref{eq:block_modelprior}, $\hat{s}_j/p_j$ corresponds to the empirical Bayes estimate of $\bs \theta$.
\begin{lem}\label{lem:empiricalbayesinterpret} For $p(M \mid \bs \theta)$ in \eqref{eq:block_modelprior}, the empirical Bayes estimator satisfies 
$$\hat{\theta}^{(j)} = \frac{1}{p_j}\sum_{i\in B_j}P(\beta_i \neq 0 \mid \bs y,\hat{\bs \theta}).$$
\end{lem}
The posterior mean estimator $\hat{\theta}^{(j)}=\hat{s}_j/p_j$ can be seen as an approximation to the fixed-point equation in Lemma \ref{lem:empiricalbayesinterpret}, where one replaces $\hat{\bs \theta}$ in the right-hand side by an initial guess (implicitly defined in Section \ref{ssec:databased_blockselection}). 

\subsection{Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:empiricalbayesinterpret}}

Denote the marginal likelihood of $\bs y$ given $\bs \theta$ by $H(\bs \theta)=p(\bs y \mid \bs \theta)$. For every $j=1,\ldots,b$, the partial derivative of its logarithm with respect to $\theta^{(j)}$ is
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:logderivative}
        \frac{\partial \ln H(\bs \theta)}{\partial \theta^{(j)}}=\frac{\partial H(\bs \theta)}{\partial \theta^{(j)}}\,H(\boldsymbol{\theta})^{-1}.
    \end{equation}
    Observe that:
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:partialmarglikelihood}
    H(\bs \theta)=\sum_{M \in \M}p(\bs y \mid M,\bs\theta)p(M \mid \bs \theta)
    \quad \text{and}\quad 
    \frac{\partial H(\bs \theta)}{\partial \theta^{(j)}}=\sum_{M\in \M}p(\bs y \mid M,\bs\theta)\frac{\partial p(M \mid \bs \theta)}{\partial \theta^{(j)}}
    \end{equation}
    Recall that each model is defined as $M=(m_1,\ldots,m_p)$ where $m_i = I(\beta_i \neq 0)$ indicates whether variable $j$ is included under $M$, and that our choice of model prior in \eqref{eq:block_modelprior} is 
    \begin{align}
        p( M \mid \boldsymbol{\theta})=
        \prod_{j=1}^b \big(\theta^{(j)}\big)^{\sum_{i \in B_j} m_i}
        \big(1 - \theta^{(j)}\big)^{p_j - \sum_{i \in B_j} m_i}.
        \nonumber
    \end{align}
    Hence, simple algebra shows that for every $M\in \mathcal M$
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:partialprobmodel}
    \frac{\partial p(M \mid \bs \theta)}{\partial \theta^{(j)}}= p(M \mid \bs \theta) \bigg(\frac{\sum_{i\in B_j}m_i}{\theta^{(j)}}-\frac{p_j-\sum_{i\in B_j}m_i}{1-\theta^{(j)}} \bigg).       
    \end{equation}
    
    Replacing \eqref{eq:partialprobmodel} into \eqref{eq:partialmarglikelihood}, and using that for any function $f$ $$\sum_{M\in \M}\sum_{i\in B_j}m_if(M)=\sum_{i\in B_j}\sum_{M\in \M:m_i=1}f(M),$$ we get
    \begin{align}\label{eq:partialmarglikelihood2}
        \frac{\partial H(\bs \theta)}{\partial \theta^{(j)}}&\;=\;\frac{1}{\theta^{(j)}}\sum_{i \in B_j}\sum_{M\in \M: m_i=1}p(\bs y \mid M,\bs\theta)p(M \mid \bs \theta) \\&\quad- \frac{1}{1-\theta^{(j)}}\bigg[p_j H(\bs \theta)-\sum_{i \in B_j}\sum_{M\in \M: m_i=1}p(\bs y \mid M,\bs\theta)p(M \mid \bs \theta). \nonumber
        \bigg]
    \end{align}
    Note that
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{\sum_{M\in \M: m_i=1}p(\bs y \mid M,\bs\theta)p(M \mid \bs \theta)}{H(\bs \theta)} = \frac{\sum_{M\in \M: m_i=1}p(\bs y , M\mid \bs \theta)}{p(\bs y \mid \bs \theta)}= P(m_i = 1\mid \bs y,\bs \theta)
    \end{align*}
    By \eqref{eq:logderivative}, we then get the following expression of the partial derivative, for every $j=1,\ldots,b$,
    $$\frac{\partial \ln H(\bs \theta)}{\partial \theta^{(j)}}=\frac{1}{\theta^{(j)}}\sum_{i \in B_j}P(m_i = 1\mid \bs y,\bs \theta) - \frac{1}{1-\theta^{(j)}}\bigg[p_j-\sum_{i \in B_j}P(m_i = 1\mid \bs y,\bs \theta)
        \bigg]$$
    Setting the partial derivatives to 0 and solving for $\theta^{(j)}$ gives the desired result.

%\renewcommand*{\theHchapter}{\thepart.\arabic{chapter}}
\section{Auxiliary results}\label{suppsec:auxiliary}

In this section we collect some technical results.

\subsection{Solutions to penalized likelihood problems}

\begin{lem}\label{lem:l1isthreshold}
	In the sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, selecting the non-zero entries  of the solution to 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genl1}
	\underset{\bs \beta \in \R^p}{\rm minimize} \;\;\tfrac12\|\bs y-\sqrt{n}\bs\beta\|^2+\sum_{j=1}^b \lambda_j \sum_{i \in B_j}|\beta_{i}|
\end{equation}
for non-negative $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_b$  is equivalent to taking the selector $\hat S^{b}$ in \eqref{prop:isthresh} with $\bs \tau=(\lambda_1/n,\ldots,\lambda_b/n)$. Let $\bs\beta^\circ$ be either the MLE or the LASSO estimator with penalty $\lambda^\circ$. Selecting the non-zero entries of the solution to the problem
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genl1ada}
	\underset{\bs \beta \in \R^p}{\rm minimize} \;\;\tfrac12\|\bs y-\sqrt{n}\bs\beta\|^2+\sum_{j=1}^b \lambda_j \sum_{i \in B_j}\tfrac{|\beta_{i}|}{|\beta_i^\circ|}.
\end{equation}
is equivalent to taking the selector $\hat S^{b}$ with $\bs \tau=(\sqrt{\lambda_1/n},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_b/n})$ if $\bs\beta^\circ$ is the MLE and with $\bs \tau=(\lambda^\circ/2n+\sqrt{(\lambda^\circ/2n)^2+\lambda_1/n},\ldots,\lambda^\circ/2n+\sqrt{(\lambda^\circ/2n)^2+\lambda_b/n})$ if $\bs\beta^\circ$ is the LASSO estimator.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
    Denote by $\hat{\bs \beta}$ the minimizer of the problem \eqref{eq:genl1}. The MLE under the full model is $\tilde{\bs\beta}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}} \bs y$ and the optimized function in \eqref{eq:genl1} can be then rewritten as
	$$
	\tfrac{1}{2}\bs y^{\top}\bs y +\tfrac{n}{2}\sum_{j=1}^b\sum_{i\in B_j}\left(\beta_i^2-2\tilde\beta_i\beta_i+2\tfrac{\lambda_j}{n}|\beta_i|\right)
	$$
	which we optimize with respect to each $\beta_i$ separately. For any $i\in B_j$ we have $\hat\beta_i=0$ if and only if $|\tilde \beta_i|\leq \lambda_j/n$. Similarly  for \eqref{eq:genl1ada}, denoting $\hat{\bs \beta}$ the minimizer of the problem, for any $i\in B_j$ we have $\hat\beta_i=0$ if and only if $|\tilde \beta_i|\leq \lambda_j/(|\beta^{\circ}_i|n)$. If $\bs \beta^{\circ}= \tilde{\bs\beta}$, then for any $i\in B_j$ $\hat{\beta}_i = 0$ if and only if $|\tilde \beta_i|\leq \sqrt{\lambda_j/n}$. If $\bs\beta^\circ$ is a LASSO estimate with penalization $\lambda^\circ$, then for any $i\in B_j$ 
 $\hat{\beta}_i = 0$ if and only if $\tilde{\beta}^2_i + \operatorname{sign}(\lambda^{\circ}/n-\tilde{\beta}_i)\lambda^\circ/(n\tilde{\beta}_i)-\lambda_j/n \leq 0$. Equivalently, for any $i\in B_j$ 
 $\hat{\beta}_i = 0$ if and only if $|\tilde{\beta}_ i| \leq  \tfrac{1}{2}\left(\lambda^\circ/n+\sqrt{(\lambda^\circ/n)^2+4 \lambda_j/n}\right)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Tail bounds}

\begin{lem}\label{lemma:3tailbounds}\textbf{Tail bounds on the maximum and minimum of folded Gaussians} 
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item If $\bs y\sim N_p(0,n^{-1}I_p)$, $p>1$, and $a \geq \sqrt{2\ln(p)/n}$, $$P\Big(\underset{i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}}{\max}|y_i|> a \Big) \leq \frac{e^{-\frac{n}{2}\left(a^2-\frac{2\ln (p)}{n}\right)}}{\sqrt{\pi\ln (p)}}.$$
    \item If $\bs y\sim N_s\left(\bs\mu,\sigma^2I_s\right)$, $s>1$, and $a \leq \underset{i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}}{\min}|\mu_i|$,
        $$P\Big(\underset{i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}}{\min}|y_i| > a\Big) \geq P\Big(\underset{i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}}{\min}|\mu_i|-\underset{i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}}{\max}|y_i-\mu_i| > a \Big).$$
    \item If $y_i\sim N\left(\mu,\sigma^2\right)$ for $i=1,\ldots,s$ are independent, and $0<a<|\mu|$, then
        $$P\Big(\underset{i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}}{\min}|y_i| > a \Big) \leq \exp\left\{-\frac{s}{2}\frac{e^{-2\sigma^{-2}(|\mu|-a)^2/\pi}-e^{-\sigma^{-2}(a+|\mu|)^2/2}}{\left(1-e^{-2\sigma^{-2}(|\mu|-a)^2/\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(1-e^{-\sigma^{-2}(a+|\mu|)^2/2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right\}.$$
\end{enumerate}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}

\textbf{Part (i)} \,\, By the union bound and the identical distribution of the $y_i$'s,
\begin{center}
    $P\Big(\underset{i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}}{\max}|y_i|> a \Big)     \leq \sum_{i=1}^{p}{ P\left(|\sqrt{n}y_i|>\sqrt{n}a \right)}
    = p\; P\left(|z|>\sqrt{n}a\right)$
\end{center}
    where $z\sim N(0,1)$. 
    By symmetry and using the standard tail bound for standard normal, $P(z \geq \delta) \leq (2\pi)^{-1/2} \delta^{-1} e^{-\delta^{2} / 2}$ for $\delta \geq 0$, we obtain that 
\begin{center}
  $P\Big(\underset{i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}}{\max}|y_i|> a \Big) 
     \leq  p\; 2 \, P\left(z>\sqrt{n}a\right) \leq \frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{n}a}p e^{-\frac{n}{2} a^{2}}.$
\end{center}
Since $a \geq \sqrt{2\ln(p)/n}$, we have that $\sqrt{2}/\sqrt{n}a\leq 1/\sqrt{\ln(p)}$. Taking $a^2=a^2-\frac{2\ln(p)}{n}+\frac{2\ln(p)}{n}$, we get \begin{center}
$ P\Big(\underset{i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}}{\max}|y_i|> a \Big) \leq \frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi\ln(p)}} e^{-\frac{n}{2} \left(a^{2}-\frac{2\ln(p)}{n}\right)}.$ \\\end{center}

\textbf{Part (ii)} Consider the events $A:=\{ \underset{i \in \{1,\ldots,s\}}{\min}|\mu_i|- \underset{i \in \{1,\ldots,s\}}{\max}|y_i-\mu_i| > a\}$ and $B:=\{\underset{i \in \{1,\ldots,s\}}{\min}|y_i| > a\}$. We first show that $A$ implies $B$. $A$ implies that, for all $i$, $(\underset{i \in \{1,\ldots,s\}}{\min}|\mu_i|) - |y_i-\mu_i| > a $.  By the triangle inequality, we have that $ -|y_i-\mu_i|\leq|y_i|-|\mu_i|$ and therefore that $$ \text{for all } i, \;\;\;(\min_{i \in \{1,\ldots,s\}}|\mu_i|) - |y_i-\mu_i| \leq  (\min_{i \in \{1,\ldots,s\}}|\mu_i|) +|y_i| - |\mu_i| < |y_i|.$$ 
Then, $A$ 
implies that, for all $i$, $|y_i| > a $, that is $B$. By the monotonicity of the probability $P$
\begin{center}$ P\Big(\underset{i \in \{1,\ldots,s\}}{\min}|\mu_i|-\underset{i \in \{1,\ldots,s\}}{\max}|y_i-\mu_i| > a\Big) \leq P\Big(\underset{i \in \{1,\ldots,s\}}{\min}|y_i| > a\Big) $. \end{center}


\textbf{Part (iii)}\,\, 
Since the $y_i$'s are independent and identically distributed we have $P(\min_{i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}}|y_i|>a)=P(|y_i|>a)^s= \exp\left(s\ln{P(|y_i|>a)}\right)$ for any $i\in\{1.\ldots,s\}$. Using that $\ln\left(1+ x \right)< x$ for $x\in(-1,0)$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:grpoih}
    P(\min_{i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}}|y_i|>a)\leq \exp\left(s(P(|y_i|>a)-1)\right).
\end{equation}
To get a bound on $P(\min_{i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}}|y_i|>a)$ it is then enough to bound $P(|y_i|>a)$ for any $i$. We have
$$P(|y_i|>a) = P(y_i>a) + P(y_i<-a) = P(z>\frac{a-\mu}{\sigma}) + P(z<-\frac{a+\mu}{\sigma})$$
where $z\sim N(0,1)$. If $\mu>0$, $P(z>\frac{a-\mu}{\sigma})  =  P(z>\frac{a-|\mu|}{\sigma})= P(z<\frac{|\mu|-a}{\sigma})$ by symmetry of the standard Gaussian, and $P(z<-\frac{a+\mu}{\sigma})=P(z<-\frac{a+|\mu|}{\sigma})$, then $P(|y_i|>a) =  P(z<\frac{|\mu|-a}{\sigma}) + P(z<-\frac{a+|\mu|}{\sigma})$.
If $\mu<0$, then $P(z>\frac{a-\mu}{\sigma})=P(z>\frac{a+|\mu|}{\sigma})=P(z<-\frac{a+|\mu|}{\sigma})$, by symmetry of the standard Gaussian, and $P(z<-\frac{a+\mu}{\sigma})=P(z<\frac{|\mu|-a}{\sigma})$. Then for any $\mu$, $P(|y_i|>a)  = P(z<\frac{|\mu|-a}{\sigma}) + P(z<-\frac{a+|\mu|}{\sigma}) $. For any $a<|\mu|$ we further have
\begin{align}
    P(|y_i|>a) &= P\Big(z<\frac{|\mu|-a}{\sigma}\Big) + 1- P\Big(z<\frac{a+|\mu|}{\sigma}\Big) \nonumber \\
    &= P(z<0)
    +P\Big(0<z<\frac{|\mu|-a}{\sigma}\Big)+ 1 - P(z<0) - P\Big(0<z<\frac{a+|\mu|}{\sigma}\Big) \nonumber \\
    &= 1+P\Big(0<z<\frac{|\mu|-a}{\sigma}\Big)-P\Big(0<z<\frac{a+|\mu|}{\sigma}\Big)\label{eq:random}.
\end{align}
From \cite{chu}, for any $\delta>0$, $\frac{1}{2}\left(1-e^{-\delta^2/2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq P(0 <z < \delta)\leq \frac{1}{2}\left(1-e^{-2\delta^2/\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}$. Thus, for any $\gamma,\delta>0$, we have that
$$ P(0<z \leq \gamma)-P(0<z \leq \delta)\leq
\frac{1}{2}\left(1-e^{-2\gamma^2/\pi}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\left(1-e^{-\delta^2/2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.$$
Applying the above to \eqref{eq:random}, we get
$$P(|y_i|>a) \leq 
1+ \frac{1}{2}\left(\left(1-e^{-2(|\mu|-a)^2/\pi\sigma^2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}-\left(1-e^{-(a+|\mu|)^2/2\sigma^2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).\\$$

Using that $x^{\frac{1}{2}}-y^{\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{x-y}{x^{\frac{1}{2}}+y^{\frac{1}{2}}}$ for $x,y>0$, we get
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:pafvih}
    P(|y_i|>a) \leq 1- \frac{e^{-2(|\mu|-a)^2/\pi\sigma^2}-e^{-(a+|\mu|)^2/2\sigma^2}}{2\left(\left(1-e^{-2(|\mu|-a)^2/\pi\sigma^2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(1-e^{-(a+|\mu|)^2/2\sigma^2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)}. 
 \end{equation}
Finally, inputting in the bound in \eqref{eq:pafvih} in \eqref{eq:grpoih} gives the desired inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{lem}\label{lem:boundrhogen}
 For any $T\subseteq S$ and $M\in\M$ such that $T\not\subseteq M$, let $Q_T=M \cup T$. The non-centrality parameter defined in \eqref{eq:muQM} satisfies: 
\begin{equation}
    \mu_{Q_TM}\; \geq \;n\,\rho(\bs X)\,\sum_{j=1}^b|T_j\setminus M_j|\,\min_{i\in T_j\setminus M_j}{\beta_{i}^*}^2.
\end{equation}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof} 
The non-centrality parameter $\mu_{Q_TM}$, as defined in \eqref{eq:muQM}, satisfies
\begin{eqnarray*}
    \mu_{Q_TM}&=&\|\big(I_n-P_M\big) \bs X_{Q_T\setminus M} \bs \beta^*_{Q_T\setminus M}\|^2\\
    &=&\|\big(I_n-P_M\big) \bs X_{T\setminus M} \bs \beta^*_{T\setminus M}\|^2\\
    &= & n {\beta^*_{T\setminus M}}^\top \left(\tfrac1n \bs X_{T\setminus M}^\top(I_n-P_M)\bs X_{T\setminus M}\right)\beta^*_{T\setminus M}\\
    &\geq & n\lambda_{\min}\left(\tfrac1n \bs X_{T\setminus M}^\top(I_n-P_M)\bs X_{T\setminus M}\right)\|\beta^*_{T\setminus M}\|^2,
\end{eqnarray*}
where the second equality follows from observing that $Q_T\setminus M=T\setminus M$. Since $T$ is a subset of $S$, by reordering columns, $X_{S\setminus M}=[X_{T\setminus M},X_{S\setminus (M\cup T)}]$ and therefore $\tfrac1n \bs X_{T\setminus M}^\top(I_n-P_M)\bs X_{T\setminus M}$ is a principal submatrix of $\tfrac1n \bs X_{S\setminus M}^\top(I_n-P_M)\bs X_{S\setminus M}$. Hence, Cauchy's interlacing theorem gives that $\lambda_{\min}\left(\tfrac1n \bs X_{T\setminus M}^\top(I_n-P_M)\bs X_{T\setminus M}\right) \geq \lambda_{\min}\left(\tfrac1n \bs X_{S\setminus M}^\top(I_n-P_M)\bs X_{S\setminus M}\right)$. Finally, by definition of $\rho(\bs X)$ in \eqref{eq:rhoX} we have that
$\lambda_{\min}\left(\tfrac1n \bs X_{S\setminus M}^\top(I_n-P_M)\bs X_{S\setminus M}\right) \geq \rho(\bs X)$, and further noting that $\|\beta^*_{T\setminus M}\|^2\geq \sum_{j=1}^b|T_j\setminus M_j|\,\min_{i\in T_j\setminus M_j}{\beta_{i}^*}^2$ gives the desired result.
\end{proof}

\begin{lem}\label{lemma:s1s3}
Let $W\sim\chi_{\nu}^2(\mu)$ with $\mu \geq 0$, then for any $w>\mu+\nu$
$$P(W > w) \leq e^{-\big(\frac{w+\mu}{2}  - \sqrt{2w( 2 \mu+ \nu)-2 \mu \nu - \nu^2 } \big)}. $$
Moreover, assume $w$, $\nu$ and $\mu$ are functions of $n$ such that $w$ is increasing, $\nu=o(w)$, and $\mu=o(w)$. Then, for any $\phi\in(0,1)$ and $n$ large enough
$$P(W > w)  \leq  e^{-\phi\frac{w}{2}}.$$
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
By \cite{birge}, Lemma 8.1 we have that for any $x>0$
$$P(W> (\nu+\mu)+2 \sqrt{(\nu+2 \mu) x}+2 x) \leq e^{-x}.$$
The function $f:x\mapsto (\nu+\mu)+2 \sqrt{(\nu+2 \mu) x}+2 x$ is  one-to-one between $\R^+$ and $( \nu+
\mu,\infty)$. 
Hence, we have that for any $w>\mu+\nu$,
$$P(W > w) \leq e^{-f^{-1}(w)} \:=\; e^{-\big(\frac{w+\mu}{2}  - \sqrt{2w(2 \mu+\nu)-2 \mu \nu - \nu^2 }\big) } .$$
Observe that
$$\frac{w+\mu}{2}  - \sqrt{2w( 2 \mu+ \nu)-2 \mu \nu - \nu^2 }= \frac{w}{2}\bigg(1+\frac{\mu}{w} -\sqrt{\frac{8(2 \mu+\nu)}{w}\bigg(1-\frac{2\nu\mu-\nu^2}{2(2w\mu+w\nu)} \bigg)}\bigg).$$
 Since $\nu=o(w)$ and $\mu=o(w)$ by assumption, we have $\frac{w+\mu}{2}  - \sqrt{2w( 2 \mu+ \nu)-2 \mu \nu - \nu^2 }= \frac{w}{2}(1+o(1))$. Therefore, for any $\phi\in(0,1)$ and every $n$ large enough.
$$P(W > w) \leq  e^{-\phi\frac{w}{2}}.$$
\end{proof}


\begin{lem}\label{lemma:s2} Let $W \sim \chi_\nu^2(\mu)$ with $\mu>0$. For any $w<\mu$, 
$$
P(W<w) \leq \frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\sqrt{\mu}-\sqrt{w})^2}}{(\mu / w)^{\nu / 4}}.$$
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
The result follows directly from \cite{rossell2022concentration}, Lemma S2.
\end{proof}

\begin{lem}\label{lemma:s18} 
    Let $W\sim \chi_\nu^2(\mu)$ with $\mu \geq 0$. Assume that $g$, $\nu$ and $\mu$ are functions of $n$ such that $g$ is positive and increasing,  $\nu=o(\ln(g))$, and $\mu=o(\ln(g))$.
